Your route to 95% practice mastery

Use this as a learning target, not an exam prediction. First attempts matter more than scores earned after memorising the answers.

  1. Understand: study one module's plain-English explanation, then its mapped lessons. Explain the control aloud without reading.
  2. Practise: answer that module's reinforcement questions. Study the explanation for every mistake and every correct guess.
  3. Apply: complete the corresponding fixture exercise, then the live Microsoft lab when you have an appropriate tenant. Record simulation and live work separately.
  4. Check: attempt Mixed check A without notes. The local target is at least 29/30 (96.7%) overall and at least 90% in each domain.
  5. Repair: return to the linked lesson for every error. Write the mistaken rule, corrected rule and one new example.
  6. Recheck: attempt Mixed check B on a later day without first reading its answers. Apply the same target. Repeated attempts are practice, not unseen evidence.
  7. Demonstrate: explain access versus discovery, policy scope versus enforcement, and user versus agent identity correctly; complete the lab evidence checklist. A high quiz score cannot cancel a critical misconception.

These small checks are uncalibrated and not replicas of the real exam. They are weighted 7 tenant, 12 security and 11 AI items each (23.3%, 40%, 36.7%), within the published domain ranges. They sample the course rather than exhaust every exam possibility.

A sustainable session

Allow about 15 minutes for the explanation, 30–45 minutes for lessons, 20–30 minutes for exercises and 15 minutes for retrieval practice. Take longer when you cannot explain why an alternative is wrong. The full 30-day schedule remains below.

Multiple-response questions require both correct choices and no incorrect choices. Unanswered questions count as incorrect. Written and matching activities remain in the reference bank and are not included in the automatic percentage.

Attempt history

  • Show allowed and denied outcomes for a resource boundary.
  • Explain a sign-in using actual or clearly labelled fixture evidence.
  • Distinguish DLP simulation from enforcement.
  • Keep an agent identity, paired user and human user separate in the design.
  • Explain what a restore check establishes and what it does not.
  • Reconcile adoption denominators and cost scope.
  • For a missing feature or pending report, record the gap rather than inventing a pass.

AB-650 | Beginner to enterprise practitioner

Independent preparation • Version 2.0 • Updated 7 October 2026 • Self-paced with optional facilitated workshops

Prepared for Anir Ghosal. Concept-first explanations, Microsoft administration examples and guided evidence collection precede commands. Your Microsoft infrastructure experience is useful, but this course assumes no prior knowledge of the newer AI and agent administration features.

Read this first

This is original, independent training material, not an official Microsoft course, exam dump or endorsed product. It contains 60 mapped lessons across 13 exam modules, a foundation bridge, practical work, 300 original assessment activities, facilitator materials and a 30-day plan. The assessment bank deliberately includes related retrieval-practice sets; it is not five independent or statistically calibrated mock exams. Matching activities are printable drag-and-drop equivalents. The package does not contain recorded videos, an LMS/SCORM package or a hosted tenant.

Plan around 60 hours over 30 study days, with extra time for unfamiliar concepts and lab access. This is a study estimate. Aim for at least 95% on this course’s practice checks, plus satisfactory practical evidence. This target is not a validated predictor of a Microsoft result, a cohort pass-rate claim or an exam-score guarantee.

How to study each lesson

  • Read the concept aloud and explain it using the fictional Northstar Consulting tenant.
  • Predict the scenario outcome before reading the recommended action.
  • Explain why each tempting alternative fails the requirement.
  • Complete the practical exercise and record evidence, not just a screenshot of a saved toggle.
  • Use the linked primary learning module for expanded product walkthroughs; return to any weak prerequisite.
  • Attempt the matching and scenario questions without notes; inspect answers only after committing.
  • Maintain an error log: lesson, mistaken assumption, corrected rule, evidence and retest date. Revisit on days 2, 7 and 14 after the initial attempt.

Lab access, costs and evidence standards

Use an isolated authorized training tenant and synthetic content. No tenant is provisioned by this package, and a free developer or trial tenant is not guaranteed. Basic administration requires the relevant Microsoft 365 workload entitlement. Advanced Purview, PIM, Defender, Copilot, Agent 365 and SharePoint controls can require additional plans, rollout eligibility or billing. Verify each feature in the current product documentation and tenant before buying anything. A read role may show a page without allowing a change.

There are two routes. Live route: perform the bounded exercise with required permissions and entitlements. Offline route: use the supplied fixtures and complete the same decision worksheet. Label each evidence item LIVE, OFFLINE or NOT TESTED. Offline reasoning is useful preparation but does not establish hands-on proficiency. Do not purchase subscriptions, enable meters, send campaigns, enforce tenant-wide policies or change production merely to complete a lab. Optional live actions need the tenant owner’s ordinary authorization.

For every lab record: requirement; resource; identity and role; license/feature check; baseline; intended change; positive test; negative test; observed outcome; propagation or uncertainty; rollback and cleanup. A role or feature described as unavailable is a finding, not permission to substitute Global Administrator. Cleanup must respect existing retention and records controls.

Foundation bridge — 3 hours

F01, 45 minutes: tenant, subscription, identity, group, role and resource. Outcome: explain why a user can sign in but cannot open a document. Exercise: sketch five objects and label the relationships. Answer: authentication proves identity, while resource authorization still depends on effective permissions. Mapping: prerequisite for D1.T1, D2.I1 and D2.A1; no claim of a separate official domain.

F02, 45 minutes: cloud workloads and data paths. Outcome: distinguish Exchange mail, Teams conversations, SharePoint shared files and OneDrive personal work files. Exercise: classify a shared mailbox, project library, private-channel file and personal draft. Ask where access is actually enforced rather than choosing by the application icon. Mapping: prerequisite for D1.W1 and D2.P1.

F03, 45 minutes: AI vocabulary. A model generates outputs; grounding supplies relevant source information; an agent combines instructions, context and tools to pursue a task; a connector links a source; a tool exposes an operation. Exercise: trace a request to summarize a document versus send its summary. The second operation adds a destination and an action requiring its own authorization. Mapping: prerequisite for D3.C1, D3.I1 and D3.G1.

F04, 45 minutes: evidence and commands. Read a command as verb, resource, parameters and target. Distinguish configuration, logs and observed behavior. Exercise: explain every line of the read-only script later in this guide before running it. Mapping: prerequisite for D2.I1.7 and all verification exercises. Foundation gate: correctly explain access versus authentication, licensing versus roles and configuration versus outcome, then identify one appropriate positive and negative test.

Current controls that are easy to confuse

M01 — Tenant foundations and licensing

UNDERSTAND FIRST · MODULE 01

Start with the organisation, then give people the right services

In simple English

A tenant is your organisation's Microsoft 365 environment. It contains accounts, settings and subscriptions. A domain is a name your organisation controls, such as northstar.example. A licence gives someone an entitlement to use a product. A role gives someone permission to administer something. These are separate ideas. Buying a licence does not make a person an administrator. Giving someone an administrator role does not buy the product.

How it works

First identify the tenant and the business requirement. Then identify the object you need to change: the organisation profile, a domain, a user, or a licence assignment. Choose the role needed for that task. For a custom domain, follow Microsoft's ownership verification process before using the domain. Keep verification separate from changing mail delivery. For group-based licensing, a group acts as an assignment list: eligible members receive the selected product licences. Assignment can fail, so membership is only the starting point of your check.

Worked example

Fictional example: Northstar adds 20 staff. All 20 join the licensing group, but only 18 receive the expected service. Do not record 100% success because the group has 20 members. Check each user's assignment result, available licences, usage location and service-plan conflicts. Success means the intended users have the intended service, not just a membership entry.

Use this process

  1. Write the requirement in one sentence: who needs which service?
  2. Record the current domain, assignment and role settings relevant to the task.
  3. Make the smallest change in the training scope.
  4. Check the assignment result and test as the intended user.
  5. Record unresolved errors separately from completed work.

Keep this distinction: Entitlement answers 'May this user use the product?' Administrative permission answers 'May this person change the product's settings?' Neither answer proves that the user's client is ready.

Stop and answer: A domain is verified, but mail must stay with the old provider for one more week. Should you change the MX record now?

Check your reasoning

No. Keep the approved mail route until the planned cutover. Verification proves control of the domain; changing MX affects delivery.

Read with the mapped lessons below. Microsoft reference

Estimated study: 3 hours. Lab prerequisites: Tenant or Entra settings administrator; license administrator for entitlement work. Use a separate test tenant.

Think of a tenant as your organization's rented office building. Identities are the people recognized by reception, domains are the addresses people use, services are the rooms where work happens, and licenses determine which facilities are available. This analogy has a limit: Microsoft 365 permissions are evaluated by several connected services, not by one receptionist. A successful sign-in does not mean every room is available.

Start with a requirement and follow its dependency chain. If the requirement is a new email domain, you need ownership verification, account and service configuration, and a deliberate routing decision. If the requirement is a recognizable login, investigate the branding surface. If a user lacks a capability, distinguish a missing product assignment from a disabled service plan. The wrong control can appear to save successfully while leaving the requirement unmet.

For troubleshooting, compare an affected user with a working user. Record entitlement sources, direct group membership and service-plan state. Avoid inferring success from a group-level message. A user moved between licensing groups needs the destination assignment verified before the previous source is removed. This is an example of preserving service continuity while changing configuration.

Key concepts: Branding, Domain verification, Organization settings, Group licensing.

Diagram to create: Create a two-layer tenant map: identity and administration above workload resources. Mark domain verification separately from mail-routing arrows.

Knowledge check with answer: Why can a verified domain still deliver mail elsewhere? Answer: verification proves ownership; routing follows the configured mail records.

Common mistakes: Confusing appearance with trust, a group assignment with every user entitlement, and a saved value with effective behavior.

Exam tip: Underline the requested outcome before choosing a portal. Domain, branding, entitlement and access are different requirements.

L01 — Branding

Objective pointer: D1.T1.1. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Branding gives users recognizable organization and sign-in experiences; it does not establish trust in a message or authorize access.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A newly acquired division needs its logo, company URL, theme and sign-in background reviewed before launch.

Recommended approach: Review Microsoft 365 organization branding and Entra sign-in branding separately, then test both experiences.

  • Alternative to reject: Change only the email signature. Why: Signatures do not configure either tenant branding experience.
  • Alternative to reject: Use the logo as evidence that a sign-in is genuine. Why: Attackers can reproduce visual branding.
  • Alternative to reject: Assign a security group to the theme. Why: Group membership is not the branding configuration.

Verification: Capture the organization experience and a fresh sign-in view using the pilot identity, checking the intended URLs and imagery.

Practical exercise: Draw two browser frames and mark which portal controls each visual element.

L02 — Domain verification

Objective pointer: D1.T1.2. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Domain ownership verification and mail routing are separate decisions. Proving control of a namespace does not itself move email.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A company owns a new domain but must keep its existing mail provider until a scheduled migration.

Recommended approach: Add the domain and publish the supplied verification record; defer mail-routing changes until the agreed cutover.

  • Alternative to reject: Change MX records immediately. Why: This can move mail before the service is ready.
  • Alternative to reject: Rename every account first. Why: Verification should precede use of an unverified namespace.
  • Alternative to reject: Create a SharePoint site with the domain name. Why: A site name does not prove DNS ownership.

Verification: Check verified domain status and independently confirm the production MX destination still matches the approved pre-cutover configuration.

Practical exercise: Prepare a DNS change ticket separating verification, routing, rollback and validation.

L03 — Organization settings

Objective pointer: D1.T1.3. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: A tenant contains organization-wide service choices and profile information; each setting needs a business owner and an understood scope.

Northstar Consulting scenario: HR requests a profile update while security requests a review of privacy and service defaults.

Recommended approach: Inventory the affected organization settings, identify owners, record current values and pilot supported scoped changes.

  • Alternative to reject: Treat every organization setting as a per-device policy. Why: Tenant and device scopes differ.
  • Alternative to reject: Use branding settings for privacy controls. Why: Appearance is not a privacy configuration.
  • Alternative to reject: Give HR permanent Global Administrator. Why: The business request does not justify unrestricted administration.

Verification: Compare the saved values and effective experience against the approved requirements, including any propagation delay.

Practical exercise: Write a change record with old value, new value, scope, owner and recovery action.

L04 — Group licensing

Objective pointer: D1.T1.4. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: A license is an entitlement assembled from service plans. Group assignment must be verified for the intended users rather than inferred from membership somewhere in a hierarchy.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A licensed parent group contains 20 direct users and a nested group of 10 users. Only the direct users receive the product.

Recommended approach: Assign the entitlement to an appropriate group containing the intended direct user members and inspect assignment results.

  • Alternative to reject: Wait for nested membership to inherit the license. Why: Nested groups are not processed by group-based licensing.
  • Alternative to reject: Enable an unrelated service plan. Why: The missing assignment is a membership-scope issue.
  • Alternative to reject: Treat group success as proof all 30 users are licensed. Why: Success covers processed users, not nested users.

Verification: Inspect direct membership, user-level entitlement sources, service-plan state and assignment errors for the ten affected accounts.

Practical exercise: Build a before-and-after table of 30 expected entitlements; simulate two insufficient-seat failures.

Primary expansion reading:
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/provision-brand-microsoft-365-tenant/
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/license-microsoft-365-ai-services/

M02 — Operational resilience and funding

UNDERSTAND FIRST · MODULE 02

Keep services working and know how to recover

In simple English

An outage is a service problem. A network problem is a connection problem. A recovery problem is an inability to restore the information you need. They can look similar to a user, but they require different evidence. Service health tells you about reported Microsoft service incidents. Connectivity information helps you investigate the path between users and Microsoft 365. Backup prepares you to recover supported data.

How it works

Begin with scope. Is the problem affecting one person, one office, one application or many locations? Compare the timing with Service health. If only one office is affected, compare network results with an unaffected office. For recovery, define what data must be protected and how recent a restored copy must be. Confirm the backup configuration and practise a permitted restore. Retention and backup serve different requirements; a rule to keep records is not proof that a restore will meet a recovery objective.

Worked example

Fictional example: Teams works at home but repeatedly disconnects at one branch. There is no matching service incident. This is evidence to investigate the branch connection; it is not proof that Microsoft has no problem anywhere. Compare affected and unaffected paths, record times, and escalate with the evidence. Separately, a finance recovery exercise fails because the required resource was never protected. A successful backup of another resource cannot close that finding.

Use this process

  1. Describe affected users, locations, services and first known failure time.
  2. Check the relevant service incident and notifications.
  3. Compare connectivity evidence instead of guessing from one symptom.
  4. For backup, check protected scope and available recovery points.
  5. Validate the restored content against the stated recovery requirement.

Keep this distinction: An incident notification is not a diagnosis of every user's problem. A configured backup is not a demonstrated restore. Pay-as-you-go funding is not the same as a user's product licence.

Stop and answer: A manager asks whether a backup is usable. Is a screenshot showing Enabled sufficient?

Check your reasoning

No. Check protection scope and recovery points, then use an authorised restore exercise to establish whether the required data can be recovered.

Read with the mapped lessons below. Microsoft reference

Estimated study: 3 hours. Lab prerequisites: Read roles for health and billing review; supported Backup role and approved billing for an optional live restore.

A service can fail at several layers. The cloud provider may have an incident; the local client may be outdated; a proxy may interfere; or the user may lack the necessary entitlement. Collect evidence that separates these hypotheses. A symptom that follows one location is a different clue from a symptom that follows one identity across locations.

Recovery starts with a business question: what must be recovered, from what time, and how will you know it is correct? A submitted restore is not the finish line. Validate the recovered content against an expected result. Agree who authorizes replacing current data and who confirms the business service works afterward.

AI administration also introduces consumption decisions. A role can allow someone to build something without paying for others to use it. Build a persona matrix before assigning products or enabling a meter. For calculations, keep the unit rate, consumption unit and period explicit. The numbers in this course are fictional teaching values, not Microsoft prices.

Key concepts: AI entitlements and funding, Backup and recovery, Network diagnosis, Service health.

Diagram to create: Draw a fault tree with client, identity, network and cloud-service branches. Add a separate recovery timeline with incident time and chosen restore point.

Knowledge check with answer: Why can a successful restore job still fail business acceptance? Answer: it may restore the wrong point, wrong scope or incomplete expected content.

Common mistakes: Confusing funding with permission, advisory scope with every failure, and retention with recovery.

Exam tip: Use the evidence that isolates the fault. A broad reset is rarely the first justified response.

L05 — AI entitlements and funding

Objective pointer: D1.T1.5. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Product access, permission to create agents and the mechanism that pays for consumption answer different questions.

Northstar Consulting scenario: Eight makers build agents for 80 occasional users. Procurement asks whether maker access alone funds all user consumption.

Recommended approach: Separate the maker roles, user product entitlements and supported consumption funding, then validate the required licensing combination.

  • Alternative to reject: Assume a maker role pays for all usage. Why: An administrative or maker permission is not a billing arrangement.
  • Alternative to reject: Assign every user an administrator role. Why: Administrative permission neither replaces licensing nor justifies broad access.
  • Alternative to reject: Use an agent owner field as billing authorization. Why: Ownership and funding are separate controls.

Verification: Produce a matrix of persona, product, role, billing source and available capacity, verified against current subscription details.

Practical exercise: Calculate a fictional monthly bill using 5000 units at 0.02 currency units each plus 80 units of fixed cost; explain the 180 total.

L06 — Backup and recovery

Objective pointer: D1.T1.6. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Backup prepares a recoverable copy or restore point for supported workloads; retention governs preservation and deletion obligations.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A test library was damaged yesterday. The recovery owner needs a defensible restoration rather than simply a retention setting.

Recommended approach: Confirm protected scope and a suitable restore point in Microsoft 365 Backup, choose the recovery option and validate recovered content.

  • Alternative to reject: Apply a new retention label and assume yesterday is restored. Why: Retention configuration does not perform point-in-time recovery.
  • Alternative to reject: Restore everything without identifying the target time. Why: An unnecessary broad restore can replace valid recent work.
  • Alternative to reject: Close the incident when the restore is submitted. Why: Submission does not establish recovery success.

Verification: Compare restored test files with known expected contents, review job status and document missing or changed items.

Practical exercise: Design a restore drill with a synthetic file, recovery target, permitted destination and acceptance checks.

L07 — Network diagnosis

Objective pointer: D1.T1.7. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Connectivity is a path from client through local network and security intermediaries to a cloud service. A healthy service can still be unreachable from one office.

Northstar Consulting scenario: Copilot works at headquarters but stalls in a branch behind a proxy; Service health shows no related advisory.

Recommended approach: Compare client connectivity at both locations and inspect the branch path, including supported Copilot traffic and WebSocket handling.

  • Alternative to reject: Reassign all user licenses. Why: A location-specific failure is not established as a licensing defect.
  • Alternative to reject: Declare a global Microsoft outage. Why: The observations isolate a branch difference.
  • Alternative to reject: Disable all network inspection permanently. Why: This is broader than the evidence supports.

Verification: Correlate timestamps, connectivity results and proxy observations, then retest the same pilot operation after the targeted fix.

Practical exercise: Create a troubleshooting table separating client, identity, network and service evidence.

L08 — Service health

Objective pointer: D1.T1.8. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Service health reports provider incidents and advisories; local symptom reports still need correlation before attribution.

Northstar Consulting scenario: Several users report a Teams problem during an advisory affecting only a different region.

Recommended approach: Compare the advisory's affected workload, scope and timing with the users' failures and configure relevant notifications.

  • Alternative to reject: Attribute every Teams ticket to the advisory. Why: Different affected scope may explain none of these failures.
  • Alternative to reject: Use the message center as definitive incident status. Why: Planned change communication and incident health serve different purposes.
  • Alternative to reject: Ignore local diagnostics because an advisory exists. Why: An unrelated local cause remains possible.

Verification: Record matching or nonmatching workload, region, time window and incident details alongside pilot tests.

Practical exercise: Draft a status update distinguishing confirmed impact, suspected cause and next diagnostic step.

Primary expansion reading:
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/license-microsoft-365-ai-services/
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/maintain-microsoft-365-tenant-health/

M03 — Collaboration and safe discovery

UNDERSTAND FIRST · MODULE 03

Understand access before you change collaboration settings

In simple English

Exchange provides email. Teams brings people into conversations and meetings. SharePoint stores shared sites and documents. OneDrive stores a user's work files. These services work together, but their permissions are not one universal switch. Opening a shared mailbox and sending as that mailbox are different permissions. Finding a document and having permission to open it are also different.

How it works

For every collaboration request, identify the resource, the person and the required action. 'Finance needs access' is incomplete. 'Priya must read the shared mailbox and send as its address' is testable. In Teams, distinguish team membership from membership of a private channel. For meeting Copilot, check the applicable meeting policy and transcription behaviour. In SharePoint, inspect effective access, including group membership and sharing links. Use discovery controls for discovery requirements; use access controls for access requirements.

Worked example

Fictional example: a project file should remain available to its approved team but should not be broadly discovered. That calls for a discovery review. If a former team member must no longer open the file, hiding it from search does not meet the requirement. Test the saved direct URL as that person. For Restricted Access Control, the permitted group condition and the underlying SharePoint permission must both be satisfied.

Use this process

  1. List the person, resource and allowed action.
  2. Inspect the actual permission route, including existing links.
  3. Check the collaboration policy at its own scope.
  4. Test an allowed user and a deliberately denied test user.
  5. Record direct access and search discovery as separate outcomes.

Keep this distinction: Full Access to a mailbox does not itself grant Send As. Restricted Content Discovery does not revoke direct access. Restricted Access Control does not itself grant the missing SharePoint permission.

Stop and answer: A user belongs to a site's RAC allowed group but has no underlying permission. Can the user open the site?

Check your reasoning

No. Both the allowed-group condition and underlying SharePoint permission are required.

Read with the mapped lessons below. Microsoft reference

Estimated study: 4 hours. Lab prerequisites: Exchange, Teams and SharePoint scoped administrators; supported Copilot and SharePoint Advanced Management entitlement for advanced live tests.

Collaboration services share identities but not every permission model. A mailbox delegate, a Teams owner and a SharePoint visitor have different capabilities. Begin with the resource and action: open, read, edit, send, share or administer. Then find the specific permission that authorizes that action. This prevents an apparently convenient broad role from becoming the default answer.

Search introduces another layer. A user can have direct access to a file without seeing it in a particular search result. Conversely, a stale search result is not proof that opening the item is still allowed. Keep access and discovery tests separate. For sensitive content, identify the grant that created unnecessary access and correct it; discovery restrictions can support an interim response but do not replace that work.

Meetings add temporal state. A policy may allow a transcript without anyone starting one. Test the organizer's option, the participant's eligibility and the actual saved artifact. In practical administration, a screenshot of a toggle is weaker evidence than the observed behavior under the right identity and meeting conditions.

Key concepts: Shared mailbox permissions, Teams membership, Meeting Copilot and transcripts, SharePoint permissions, Discovery versus access.

Diagram to create: Draw user → permission → resource, with a separate discovery branch. Make a table comparing mailbox read and send permissions.

Knowledge check with answer: Can hiding a site in broad search remove a saved link permission? Answer: no; test and repair the underlying access separately.

Common mistakes: Assuming channel membership is universal, Full Access includes Send As, or transcription allowed means a saved transcript exists.

Exam tip: Watch verbs: open, send, discover and retain imply different controls.

L09 — Shared mailbox permissions

Objective pointer: D1.W1.1. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Opening a mailbox and sending with its identity require different delegated permissions.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A delegate can read the support mailbox but cannot send a reply that appears to come directly from support.

Recommended approach: Verify the intended sending behavior and grant Send As for that mailbox to the authorized delegate.

  • Alternative to reject: Grant Full Access again. Why: Read access does not itself confer Send As.
  • Alternative to reject: Choose Send on Behalf when the sender must appear solely as support. Why: That permission exposes the delegate-on-behalf relationship.
  • Alternative to reject: Enable direct sign-in using a shared password. Why: Delegated access avoids a shared interactive credential.

Verification: Send a synthetic message through the delegate and inspect the recipient-visible sender, while confirming existing read access.

Practical exercise: Compare Full Access, Send As and Send on Behalf using three fictional helpdesk requirements.

L10 — Teams membership

Objective pointer: D1.W1.2. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Teams owners manage the workspace; channel membership and associated content boundaries depend on the channel type.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A project has a team-wide channel and a private budget channel. A new team member must not see budget discussion.

Recommended approach: Verify the channel type and manage the private channel membership separately from general team membership.

  • Alternative to reject: Make the new member a team owner to hide budget data. Why: Elevating administrative rights is not a restriction.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume every channel has identical membership. Why: Private channels have distinct membership.
  • Alternative to reject: Change the channel name to Confidential. Why: Naming does not enforce access.

Verification: Test discussion and associated file access with an included and an excluded pilot identity.

Practical exercise: Draw a team with standard, private and shared channels; mark the membership and file boundaries.

L11 — Meeting Copilot and transcripts

Objective pointer: D1.W1.3. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Meeting policy, organizer options and the existence of a transcript jointly determine the experience. Allowing transcription is not evidence that it ran.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A meeting needs transcript-grounded Copilot help after the call, but nobody started transcription.

Recommended approach: Use a supported transcript-required configuration and ensure an authorized participant starts transcription during the test meeting.

  • Alternative to reject: Assume allowing transcription creates a transcript. Why: An allowed capability is not a completed action.
  • Alternative to reject: Use only live-meeting Copilot and promise a saved transcript. Why: Live assistance can operate without a saved transcript.
  • Alternative to reject: Treat the default Off value as an immutable organizer ban. Why: The documented default can be overridden by the organizer.

Verification: Check effective meeting settings and the actual saved transcript and post-meeting experience with an eligible licensed participant.

Practical exercise: Prepare a four-row matrix for policy default, organizer override, transcript started and after-meeting availability.

L12 — SharePoint permissions

Objective pointer: D1.W1.4. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Access is the result of effective permissions, group membership and sharing links, not the folder's reassuring name.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A finance site is called Private Finance but an all-staff group still has read access.

Recommended approach: Trace effective access and remove the unintended grant or link while preserving the finance team's required access.

  • Alternative to reject: Exclude the site from search and declare the access fixed. Why: Discovery suppression does not remove permissions.
  • Alternative to reject: Change the title to Highly Confidential. Why: A name does not alter authorization.
  • Alternative to reject: Remove only a navigation link. Why: Users may still open saved URLs.

Verification: Use direct URLs with a finance identity and a nonfinance identity, recording an allow and a deny after the change.

Practical exercise: Build a permissions inventory with grant source, business owner and retain/remove decision.

L13 — Discovery versus access

Objective pointer: D1.W1.5. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Restricted discovery reduces broad surfacing; access restrictions decide who can open content. These controls need different tests.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A site should stay accessible to its permitted users by direct link but should not be broadly surfaced through organization-wide Copilot discovery.

Recommended approach: Evaluate Restricted Content Discovery for the site and separately keep reviewing its underlying permissions.

  • Alternative to reject: Remove every authorized user's permissions. Why: That violates the requirement to preserve direct access.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume a missing search result proves access is denied. Why: Discovery and authorization are separate observations.
  • Alternative to reject: Use a site restriction as proof OneDrive is identically configured. Why: Control support and scope differ by workload.

Verification: Record direct access and broad discovery separately; account for indexing delay and documented discovery exceptions.

Practical exercise: Compare an interim discovery restriction with permanent permission cleanup using two acceptance criteria.

Primary expansion reading:
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/configure-exchange-teams/
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/govern-sharepoint-onedrive-copilot/

M04 — Identity administration at scale

UNDERSTAND FIRST · MODULE 04

Give each identity only the access it needs

In simple English

An identity represents a person or another actor in a system. A user account can sign in. A guest supports external collaboration. A mail contact is an address-book entry for an external recipient; it is not an employee login. A group collects identities for a purpose. An administrator role grants management capabilities. Choose the object that fits the job before you create it.

How it works

Treat joiners, movers and leavers as a lifecycle. Create the right account, assign needed access, change it when responsibilities change, and remove access when it is no longer justified. Use Privileged Identity Management, or PIM, for supported temporary privileged access rather than assuming everyone needs a permanent role. An administrative unit can limit supported directory administration to selected objects. It does not create a separate Microsoft 365 tenant. Microsoft Graph PowerShell lets you perform supported directory tasks in bulk, but you still need the relevant permissions and a clear target list.

Worked example

Fictional example: a helpdesk team should manage only the Melbourne office's supported user tasks. An administrative unit with an appropriately scoped role fits this delegation question. It does not mean Melbourne documents are now isolated from all other staff. That is a separate data-permissions question. Before a bulk change to 100 users, inspect a small sample and the exact target list; a loop repeats mistakes just as efficiently as correct actions.

Use this process

  1. Choose user, guest, contact or group from the business need.
  2. Select a supported role and scope for the administrator's task.
  3. For temporary privilege, check eligibility, activation and expiry.
  4. For bulk work, inspect the input and preview the selected identities.
  5. Verify individual outcomes, errors and the audit trail.

Keep this distinction: A group has a purpose; its name alone does not grant a role. PIM eligibility is not the same as an active assignment. Administrative units scope supported administration, not all resource access.

Stop and answer: An external accountant needs an address-book entry only. Must you create a licensed employee account?

Check your reasoning

No. A mail contact may meet the address-book requirement. If the person also needs to collaborate, evaluate guest access separately.

Read with the mapped lessons below. Microsoft reference

Estimated study: 5 hours. Lab prerequisites: Supported User, Groups or Exchange roles for the task; Privileged Role Administrator for PIM configuration where authorized; read-only Graph permissions for inventory.

Identity management is a lifecycle. A joiner needs correct attributes and scoped resources. A mover may need old access removed as well as new access added. A leaver requires both access revocation and continuity for the work they owned. These needs can conflict if deletion happens before ownership and preservation are assessed.

Delegation answers two questions: what operation can the administrator perform, and over which objects? Roles address capability; administrative-unit scope can limit supported directory administration. PIM adds another dimension: when the privileged role is active. None of these automatically grants or isolates all workload content.

PowerShell is useful when the same administrative operation must be repeated predictably. You do not need to become a software developer to read a command, inspect its target and understand its permission requirements. Start with reads. For writes, validate the target list, pilot a small sample, handle errors per record and reconcile expected versus actual changes. A command that continues after one error can leave a partial result requiring deliberate follow-up.

Key concepts: User lifecycle, Guests and external collaboration, Group purpose, Privileged roles and PIM, Administrative units, Contacts, Graph PowerShell bulk administration.

Diagram to create: Draw joiner → mover → leaver with access and ownership checks at each transition; add capability, scope and time as three role dimensions.

Knowledge check with answer: Why is a successful Graph connection not proof that 200 updates succeeded? Answer: it validates a session, not each target operation.

Common mistakes: Equating contacts with sign-in users, confusing role eligibility with activation, and treating administrative units as separate tenants.

Exam tip: For bulk questions, check input validity, permission scope, partial failure and verification.

L14 — User lifecycle

Objective pointer: D2.I1.1. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: A user identity needs controlled creation, change and retirement. Departure handling must consider sessions, data and business ownership.

Northstar Consulting scenario: An employee leaves today but their project documents must remain available to the approved successor.

Recommended approach: Apply the approved offboarding sequence for sign-in and sessions, preserve required data and transfer ownership before destructive cleanup.

  • Alternative to reject: Delete the account immediately without checking dependencies. Why: That can disrupt continuity and retention obligations.
  • Alternative to reject: Keep sign-in active until every file is transferred. Why: The departure access risk remains.
  • Alternative to reject: Transfer the departed user's password. Why: Ownership transfer should not require sharing credentials.

Verification: Verify access revocation and successor access separately, with a record of preserved data and remaining dependencies.

Practical exercise: Write joiner, mover and leaver checklists for a fictional analyst.

L15 — Guests and external collaboration

Objective pointer: D2.I1.2. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: A guest can participate through a governed external identity; external chat capability is not equivalent to membership or file access.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A supplier needs one project site's files for six weeks but no general tenant access.

Recommended approach: Use a scoped guest collaboration arrangement with the resource owner's approval, an end-date review and the necessary resource permissions.

  • Alternative to reject: Enable broad external chat and assume files are accessible. Why: Communication reachability does not grant site permissions.
  • Alternative to reject: Share an employee account. Why: That removes individual accountability.
  • Alternative to reject: Add the supplier to an all-company group. Why: That grants unnecessary scope.

Verification: Test the intended resource and a prohibited resource with the guest, and record the review date.

Practical exercise: Write a guest access request with sponsor, resource, duration and removal conditions.

L16 — Group purpose

Objective pointer: D2.I1.3. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Security groups and Microsoft 365 groups support different management and collaboration needs. Membership changes can affect multiple connected resources.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A manager wants shared collaboration resources rather than only an access-control list.

Recommended approach: Choose a Microsoft 365 group where its connected collaboration resources satisfy the requirement, then assign accountable owners.

  • Alternative to reject: Create only a mail contact. Why: A contact is not a collaborative membership container.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume every security group provisions a shared workspace. Why: Security groups do not automatically provide the same collaboration resources.
  • Alternative to reject: Let the group operate without an owner. Why: Membership and lifecycle decisions need accountability.

Verification: Inspect the group type, owners, membership and the expected connected resources.

Practical exercise: Classify four requests as collaboration, authorization, distribution or contact management.

L17 — Privileged roles and PIM

Objective pointer: D2.I1.4. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Least privilege limits capability; time-bound activation reduces how long privileged capability remains available.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A support engineer needs elevated administration for an approved one-hour maintenance task.

Recommended approach: Use the least-privileged eligible role and the configured PIM activation workflow for the maintenance window.

  • Alternative to reject: Assign permanent Global Administrator. Why: The task does not justify permanent tenant-wide power.
  • Alternative to reject: Grant ownership of an unrelated team. Why: Team ownership does not provide the administrative role.
  • Alternative to reject: Treat role eligibility as permanent activation. Why: Eligible and active are distinct states.

Verification: Inspect activation duration, approval or justification evidence and the role state after expiry.

Practical exercise: Write an activation request identifying task, role, time window, approver and validation.

L18 — Administrative units

Objective pointer: D2.I1.5. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: An administrative unit can scope supported delegated directory administration; it is not a separate tenant or a universal data boundary.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A regional helpdesk should administer supported user tasks for its region without gaining the same scope everywhere.

Recommended approach: Use an administrative unit and a supported role assignment scoped to that unit.

  • Alternative to reject: Rename the region's users. Why: Naming alone does not scope a role.
  • Alternative to reject: Use a tenant-wide role and ask staff to avoid other users. Why: A written request is not enforced scoping.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume the unit isolates all SharePoint data. Why: Directory administration scope is not universal workload isolation.

Verification: Test a permitted operation on an in-scope test user and its rejection on an out-of-scope test user.

Practical exercise: Draw a tenant with two administrative units and annotate the supported delegated operations.

L19 — Contacts

Objective pointer: D2.I1.6. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: A mail contact represents an external recipient in the directory; it is not an interactive employee account.

Northstar Consulting scenario: Staff need to find a vendor's external email address in the address book, with no tenant sign-in.

Recommended approach: Create or manage a mail contact with the correct external address and display details.

  • Alternative to reject: Create a privileged member account. Why: The request needs neither sign-in nor administration.
  • Alternative to reject: Assign a Copilot license to the contact. Why: A directory contact is not an interactive licensed user.
  • Alternative to reject: Invite a guest solely to populate this address entry. Why: That adds an identity relationship beyond the stated need.

Verification: Verify address-book resolution and the external delivery address without creating sign-in access.

Practical exercise: Compare a contact, guest and member using purpose, sign-in and resource access columns.

L20 — Graph PowerShell bulk administration

Objective pointer: D2.I1.7. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Graph PowerShell exposes directory operations as commands. Safe bulk work uses explicit scope, validated input and per-record results.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A CSV contains duplicate user principal names and a bulk change is planned for 200 accounts.

Recommended approach: Validate and deduplicate the input, preview the exact target set, then pilot the authorized operation and log each result.

  • Alternative to reject: Run the entire write immediately. Why: Invalid targets can cause partial or unintended changes.
  • Alternative to reject: Treat a successful connection as proof all writes succeeded. Why: Authentication says nothing about each record.
  • Alternative to reject: Request all directory write permissions for a read-only inventory. Why: The permission scope exceeds the operation.

Verification: Reconcile input targets with successful, failed and skipped records, and sample the resulting directory state.

Practical exercise: Use the supplied read-only Graph exercise and design a validation report for a proposed bulk write.

Primary expansion reading:
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/provision-govern-identities-entra/

M05 — Authentication and access decisions

UNDERSTAND FIRST · MODULE 05

Work out why a sign-in succeeds or fails

In simple English

Authentication checks who is signing in. Authorisation decides what that identity can do. Multifactor authentication, or MFA, asks for more than one type of proof. Self-service password reset, or SSPR, helps eligible users recover access. Conditional Access applies rules to sign-ins. A rule can require stronger proof, require a supported device condition, or block access.

How it works

Read a Conditional Access policy in three parts: who it applies to, which resource and conditions it covers, and what it requires. Then evaluate every applicable policy. An applicable block is not cancelled by a different policy that permits access after MFA. Report-only mode records what the policy would do; it does not enforce the block. A user who sees no new MFA prompt may already have satisfied MFA in an existing session. Check the sign-in event before diagnosing a bypass.

Worked example

Fictional example: one enforced policy requires MFA and another enforced policy blocks the same sign-in. The user completes MFA. Access is still blocked. The block policy applies independently. If the block policy were report-only instead, it would not enforce that block, although another policy might still deny the request. Your evidence must identify the actual policy state and the event, not just the names of two policies.

Use this process

  1. Identify the user, resource, client and time of the sign-in.
  2. Check authentication-method availability and registration where relevant.
  3. Read each matching Conditional Access result and policy state.
  4. Inspect authentication details, device information and the final result.
  5. Use a controlled test after correcting the specific problem.

Keep this distinction: Allowed authentication method does not mean registered method. Registered method does not mean it was used in this event. Sign-in risk concerns a sign-in; user risk concerns possible account compromise.

Stop and answer: A report-only block policy shows Failure. Did that policy block the user?

Check your reasoning

No. It indicates that the policy would block if enforced. Check the final event and other policies to explain what actually happened.

Read with the mapped lessons below. Microsoft reference

Estimated study: 5 hours. Lab prerequisites: Authentication Administrator or appropriate policy role; Conditional Access Administrator for a dedicated sandbox; required Entra licensing.

Authentication asks who is presenting themselves. Authorization asks what that identity may do. A stronger authentication method helps establish identity, but it does not automatically give the person access to a particular site. Keep these questions separate when interpreting a support ticket.

For policy troubleshooting, establish applicability before outcome. Which identity, resource and client are involved? Is the policy enabled or report-only? Did its conditions match? An applicable policy that requires an additional condition must be satisfied along with the other applicable policies. A block cannot be canceled by a successful MFA step elsewhere.

Logs are stronger than impressions. No new prompt may mean an earlier authentication claim satisfied the requirement, not that the control failed. Record the specific event and its authentication details. When piloting policy changes, use supported report-only analysis first, preserve a tested recovery route and limit the affected population. The aim is evidence of the intended decision, including a negative test, rather than merely a successful login.

Key concepts: Authentication methods, Password Protection, Self-service password reset, Sign-in investigation, Conditional Access interaction, Risk-based access.

Diagram to create: Draw a policy decision tree: in scope? conditions match? report-only or enforced? applicable block? required controls satisfied?

Knowledge check with answer: What does report-only failure establish? Answer: the policy evaluation indicates a failure under its logic, but report-only did not enforce blocking.

Common mistakes: Treating enablement as registration, no prompt as no MFA, or the newest Conditional Access policy as the winner.

Exam tip: Build a small truth table. Evaluate every applicable policy before predicting the final outcome.

L21 — Authentication methods

Objective pointer: D2.A1.1. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Authentication establishes identity. Method availability, registration and actual use must be distinguished.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A pilot group is allowed a stronger sign-in method, but several users have never registered it.

Recommended approach: Verify method policy scope and user registration, then run a controlled sign-in using the intended method.

  • Alternative to reject: Assume policy enablement registers everyone. Why: Users may still need registration.
  • Alternative to reject: Reset every password as proof of stronger authentication. Why: A reset does not demonstrate the desired method.
  • Alternative to reject: Use a sensitivity label to require the method. Why: Content classification does not configure sign-in methods.

Verification: Inspect the effective method policy, registration status and authentication details for the pilot sign-in.

Practical exercise: Build a matrix of allowed, registered and actually used methods for three fictional users.

L22 — Password Protection

Objective pointer: D2.A1.2. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Password Protection reduces weak password choices using prohibited-password evaluation; it does not replace multifactor authentication.

Northstar Consulting scenario: Users choose passwords based on the company name. Security wants that predictable pattern rejected.

Recommended approach: Evaluate the custom banned password list and Password Protection configuration, then test controlled synthetic password changes.

  • Alternative to reject: Use an email anti-spam policy. Why: It does not evaluate password selection.
  • Alternative to reject: Disable MFA after adding banned words. Why: Password quality and additional authentication protect different risks.
  • Alternative to reject: Publish a password guideline only. Why: A guideline alone does not enforce rejection.

Verification: Record that prohibited test choices are rejected and acceptable synthetic choices work, without logging passwords.

Practical exercise: Write a password-policy test plan using placeholders rather than real credentials.

L23 — Self-service password reset

Objective pointer: D2.A1.3. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: SSPR requires an eligible, in-scope user with sufficient usable registered methods. Configuration and readiness are separate.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A pilot user is in the SSPR group but cannot complete reset because the required recovery methods are missing.

Recommended approach: Check SSPR scope, licensing and registration requirements, then resolve the missing usable method registration.

  • Alternative to reject: Add the user to a Teams channel. Why: Channel membership is unrelated to SSPR readiness.
  • Alternative to reject: Disable all recovery requirements. Why: This removes the intended assurance rather than fixing registration.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume group membership alone guarantees reset. Why: The user must also satisfy the configured method requirements.

Verification: Complete a controlled reset for the test identity and inspect the reset or audit evidence.

Practical exercise: Create a troubleshooting decision tree for out-of-scope, unregistered and method-unavailable users.

L24 — Sign-in investigation

Objective pointer: D2.A1.4. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: A missing MFA prompt does not establish a bypass; an existing authentication claim can satisfy the requirement.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A user signs in without a new prompt and the helpdesk claims Conditional Access failed.

Recommended approach: Inspect the specific sign-in's policy evaluation, authentication details and session context before changing controls.

  • Alternative to reject: Disable the policy because no prompt appeared. Why: Prompt appearance alone is insufficient evidence.
  • Alternative to reject: Treat the user's successful desktop login as the cloud sign-in record. Why: Different events may have different evaluations.
  • Alternative to reject: Create another identical MFA policy immediately. Why: Duplication can obscure the diagnosis.

Verification: Capture the relevant sign-in ID and explain each applicable policy's result and how authentication was satisfied.

Practical exercise: Annotate a synthetic log: MFA satisfied by prior claim, policy applied, final result success.

L25 — Conditional Access interaction

Objective pointer: D2.A1.5. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Applicable Conditional Access requirements are cumulative; an applicable block is not canceled by another policy's grant.

Northstar Consulting scenario: One applicable policy requires MFA; a second applicable policy blocks the user's access to the same resource.

Recommended approach: Predict blocked access and use the sign-in evidence to verify both policy evaluations.

  • Alternative to reject: Assume passing MFA overrides the block. Why: A grant does not negate an applicable block.
  • Alternative to reject: Apply only the most recently edited policy. Why: Policies are not evaluated as a last-edit-wins list.
  • Alternative to reject: Ignore resource targeting. Why: Applicability depends on assignments and conditions.

Verification: Inspect the final blocked result and the contribution of each applicable policy for the same token request.

Practical exercise: Solve a four-row policy matrix with user, resource, conditions and grant or block outcomes.

L26 — Risk-based access

Objective pointer: D2.A1.5. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Sign-in risk concerns a particular authentication event; user risk concerns potential compromise of the identity. Remediation must match the signal and supported controls.

Northstar Consulting scenario: Security sees a suspicious sign-in and separately an account marked at risk; the administrator needs a scoped response.

Recommended approach: Distinguish the risk signals, check supported licensing and policy conditions, and validate the intended response in a pilot.

  • Alternative to reject: Treat every risk signal as the same event. Why: User and sign-in risk describe different assessments.
  • Alternative to reject: Use an unrelated DLP alert as proof of sign-in risk. Why: The signal must be verified in the identity evidence.
  • Alternative to reject: Require an agent identity to complete a human MFA prompt. Why: Nonhuman identities cannot satisfy interactive remediation.

Verification: Record the risk type, matched policy, intended action and observed test outcome without assuming every risk means confirmed compromise.

Practical exercise: Write separate handling plans for a risky sign-in and a potentially compromised user.

Primary expansion reading:
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/implement-authentication-access-entra/

M06 — Threat protection and response

UNDERSTAND FIRST · MODULE 06

Investigate threats with a timeline, not a guess

In simple English

Microsoft Defender for Office 365 helps protect email and collaboration. A threat policy defines protection behaviour. An alert says something needs investigation. An incident can bring related evidence together. An attack simulation is a controlled learning exercise, not proof that a real attack happened. These are connected tools, but each answers a different question.

How it works

Start by understanding which protection applies to the recipient and workload. Policy scope and precedence matter: a policy that exists but does not apply cannot explain protection for that person. When an alert appears, inspect the message, recipients, delivery outcome and related events. Separate 'message delivered', 'user clicked' and 'account compromised'. Each claim needs evidence. Choose response actions that match the finding and your response authority. Check the result before closing the incident.

Worked example

Fictional example: a harmful email reaches six mailboxes. One recipient clicks its link. This supports investigating that recipient and the message's impact; it does not establish that all six passwords were stolen. Remove or contain confirmed harmful content through the appropriate response process, investigate the clicked user's sign-ins, and record unknowns. A training campaign later tests the behaviour you want to improve, such as reporting suspicious messages.

Use this process

  1. Determine the affected recipients and effective protection settings.
  2. Build a timeline from delivery, click and identity evidence.
  3. State what is confirmed, suspected and still unknown.
  4. Select and verify proportionate remediation.
  5. Design follow-up training around the observed behaviour.

Keep this distinction: An alert is a lead, not the whole investigation. Deleting a message does not establish that identity risk has been resolved. A simulation completion count does not prove safer behaviour by itself.

Stop and answer: A link click is confirmed. Is account takeover confirmed too?

Check your reasoning

No. Investigate additional evidence. The click is important, but it does not prove successful credential theft or use.

Read with the mapped lessons below. Microsoft reference

Estimated study: 4 hours. Lab prerequisites: Appropriate Defender security read or response role; Defender for Office 365 Plan 2 or equivalent entitlement for advanced investigation and simulation features.

Security operations move from signal to scope to action. An alert is a starting point, not a complete narrative. Ask what happened, what evidence supports it, which identities or resources are involved and what remains unknown. Record the action and the outcome separately so another analyst can reproduce the decision.

Threat policies have their own evaluation behavior. A priority number is meaningful within the relevant policy type, not as a universal ordering of all security controls. A setting effective for spam is not necessarily the control required for impersonation. Examine feature, policy source, recipients and settings together.

Human learning is part of prevention. A simulation should have a defined population, approval, proportionate content and useful follow-up. Avoid turning a learning exercise into a public ranking of staff. Improvement is better assessed through reporting and repeated behavior than through a single delivery or click count alone. The fictional exercises here use no real malicious links or credentials.

Key concepts: Alert triage, Threat policy scope, Threat policy precedence, Threat investigation and response, Attack simulation training.

Diagram to create: Draw an incident evidence chain from alert to message to affected identity to remediation result. Add a separate per-feature policy ladder.

Knowledge check with answer: Why does closing an alert not prove containment? Answer: status may change without removing the message or addressing compromised access.

Common mistakes: Assuming custom priority zero beats a preset, every recipient was compromised, or simulation delivery proves learning.

Exam tip: Choose the response proportional to the verified scope; preserve the distinction between suspicion and evidence.

L27 — Alert triage

Objective pointer: D2.S1.1. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: An alert is a signal requiring assessment; its status is not the same as proof that the underlying threat was removed.

Northstar Consulting scenario: An email alert was marked resolved, but the analyst has not checked whether the delivered messages remain in mailboxes.

Recommended approach: Verify the evidence, affected recipients and remediation outcome before treating the threat as contained.

  • Alternative to reject: Use the resolved status as proof of message removal. Why: Workflow status and remediation are different facts.
  • Alternative to reject: Delete all recent email. Why: That is disproportionate and destroys legitimate content.
  • Alternative to reject: Reassign a license to close the alert. Why: Licensing does not resolve the threat evidence.

Verification: Link the alert to message or incident evidence and the recorded remediation result.

Practical exercise: Prepare an incident note with signal, scope, action, outcome and unresolved questions.

L28 — Threat policy scope

Objective pointer: D2.S1.2. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Threat-protection settings must match the feature and recipients. A policy's name does not prove effective coverage.

Northstar Consulting scenario: Finance needs protection against impersonation, but the administrator changed only a spam threshold.

Recommended approach: Review the anti-phishing controls relevant to impersonation and their effective recipient scope.

  • Alternative to reject: Increase backup frequency. Why: Backup does not stop the impersonation message.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume a spam threshold configures every phishing control. Why: Different protection features have different settings.
  • Alternative to reject: Change the display name of the finance group. Why: A name does not enable threat protection.

Verification: Confirm the applicable protection feature, protected identities or domains where relevant and effective recipients.

Practical exercise: Build a threat-to-control table covering malicious links, attachments, impersonation and spam.

L29 — Threat policy precedence

Objective pointer: D2.S1.2. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Custom policy priority orders comparable custom policies; applicable preset protection can take precedence for a feature.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A priority-zero custom anti-spam policy seems ignored for a user included in Standard preset protection.

Recommended approach: Inspect feature-specific precedence and recipient inclusion in the preset before changing custom priority.

  • Alternative to reject: Set custom priority to zero again. Why: It already has that priority and does not override the preset.
  • Alternative to reject: Merge every policy's settings mentally. Why: Lower settings are not simply blended into the winning policy.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume one global winner applies identically to every feature. Why: Precedence is evaluated by protection feature.

Verification: Identify the actual policy source for the feature and explain why it wins for that recipient.

Practical exercise: Write a policy-evaluation worksheet for two recipients with different preset membership.

L30 — Threat investigation and response

Objective pointer: D2.S1.3. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Investigation connects evidence to affected assets; response must record what action was taken and whether it worked.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A phishing message reached multiple users and one user reports clicking its link.

Recommended approach: Investigate message and related identity evidence, scope affected users, and carry out the authorized targeted response.

  • Alternative to reject: Remove only the original alert. Why: The underlying exposure remains.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume every recipient's device is compromised. Why: Delivery is not proof of compromise.
  • Alternative to reject: Send the suspicious message to all staff for inspection. Why: That spreads potentially harmful content.

Verification: Correlate message identifiers, recipient evidence, click or sign-in signals where available and the result of each remediation action.

Practical exercise: Build an incident timeline using fictional delivery, click, sign-in and remediation times.

L31 — Attack simulation training

Objective pointer: D2.S1.4. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: A simulation is a controlled learning activity with approved scope and follow-up; it is not permission to send uncontrolled phishing.

Northstar Consulting scenario: The organization wants to improve reporting behavior after a phishing incident.

Recommended approach: Plan an approved simulation for a bounded population with appropriate training and measurable follow-up.

  • Alternative to reject: Use real stolen credentials in the exercise. Why: Training does not require real credential compromise.
  • Alternative to reject: Measure success only by messages delivered. Why: Delivery does not show learning or reporting improvement.
  • Alternative to reject: Send an unapproved simulation to all customers. Why: That exceeds the training population and authorization.

Verification: Compare reporting and repeat-behavior measures across the planned exercise and follow-up, noting cohort differences.

Practical exercise: Draft a simulation plan with audience, owner, scenario, communications and learning measures.

Primary expansion reading:
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/secure-email-collaboration-defender-office365/

M07 — Information protection and governance

UNDERSTAND FIRST · MODULE 07

Give each data-protection control one clear job

In simple English

Classification identifies sensitive content. A sensitivity label describes a classification and can apply configured protection. A label policy makes labels available to selected people. Data loss prevention, or DLP, controls supported handling of sensitive content. Retention governs how long content is kept or when it is deleted. These controls complement one another; they do not all do the same job.

How it works

Write a data-protection requirement as location, condition and action. Where is the risk: email, a file, a Teams message, a device or a supported Copilot interaction? What should match: a sensitive information type, a label or another supported condition? What must happen: audit, notify or restrict a supported action? Confirm that the selected workload supports the combination. Simulation records matches without enforcing the action. Publishing a sensitivity label does not automatically label every existing document.

Worked example

Fictional example: finance must keep a record for an approved period and prevent inappropriate sharing. Retention addresses the keep/delete requirement. Permissions, configured label protection and applicable DLP address access and handling requirements. One control is not evidence that the other requirement is met. Use synthetic matching content and a near-match to check detection; then inspect the exact event and policy mode.

Use this process

  1. State the data, location, intended recipients and business requirement.
  2. Choose classification, sensitivity protection, DLP or retention by purpose.
  3. Check supported conditions, actions and feature entitlement.
  4. Test a true match and a near-match in the training scope.
  5. Investigate alerts and document confirmed enforcement separately from simulation.

Keep this distinction: A sensitivity label is not necessarily encryption: its configuration matters. Retention is not DLP. A Copilot policy is not universal protection for every agent and every upload.

Stop and answer: A DLP simulation reports a match. Can you claim the data transfer was blocked?

Check your reasoning

No. A match is detection evidence. Simulation does not enforce the blocking action.

Read with the mapped lessons below. Microsoft reference

Estimated study: 6 hours. Lab prerequisites: Purview read roles for inspection; appropriate DLP, information-protection and lifecycle roles for isolated policy work; feature-specific licensing.

Start with what the business needs to protect and what must happen. Classification identifies meaningful content. A label can communicate its sensitivity and apply configured protection. DLP governs supported actions involving sensitive content. Retention governs how long content must remain and how disposal is handled. Backup supports recovery. One product family may expose several of these, but their purposes are not interchangeable.

Detection quality matters. A broad pattern may find both real identifiers and harmless lookalikes. A known roster suggests a different approach from free-form narrative documents. Test positives and negatives. If a policy catches everything, it may block necessary work and train users to ignore warnings; if it catches too little, the apparent simplicity hides risk.

For each workload, specify location, condition and action. Do not project an endpoint action onto a Teams message or assume a Copilot rule covers every agent channel. Treat preview capabilities and licensing as explicit prerequisites. When reviewing alerts, establish whether an action was attempted, allowed or blocked. When reviewing posture findings, assign owners and validate remediation rather than assuming the dashboard repaired the issue.

Key concepts: Classification techniques, Sensitivity labels and publishing, DLP requirements, Copilot DLP boundaries, Retention design, DLP alert response, DSPM for AI.

Diagram to create: Draw five labeled boxes: classify, protect, restrict action, preserve, recover. Connect each to a different business requirement.

Knowledge check with answer: Why is a DLP alert not automatically proof of a leak? Answer: it may describe a prevented attempt; inspect the action outcome.

Common mistakes: Confusing sensitivity with retention, label creation with publication, a citation with processing, and posture visibility with remediation.

Exam tip: Translate the scenario into data shape, location, action and time. Those four clues usually narrow the control.

L32 — Classification techniques

Objective pointer: D2.P1.2. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Pattern detection, matching known records and recognizing document meaning address different classification problems.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A control must match actual customer identifiers from a maintained roster rather than every string shaped like an identifier.

Recommended approach: Evaluate exact data match against the approved reference dataset and test both known and unknown synthetic values.

  • Alternative to reject: Use only a broad regular expression. Why: It recognizes shape rather than membership in the roster.
  • Alternative to reject: Use a retention period as the detector. Why: Preservation duration does not identify sensitive values.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume any classifier has zero false positives. Why: Detection quality needs measurement.

Verification: Test known records, lookalike nonrecords and realistic benign content, recording false positives and false negatives.

Practical exercise: Compare pattern-based SIT, exact data match and trainable classification for three sample data shapes.

L33 — Sensitivity labels and publishing

Objective pointer: D2.P1.2. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: A sensitivity label describes and can protect content; a publishing policy makes labels available to the intended users. Encryption depends on configuration.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A new label exists, but the pilot users cannot select it in their supported applications.
