Recommended approach: Check its publishing policy, scope and client availability, then validate both label selection and any configured protection.

  • Alternative to reject: Recreate the label repeatedly. Why: The issue may be distribution rather than definition.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume every label encrypts every file. Why: Protection depends on label configuration and workload.
  • Alternative to reject: Apply a retention label instead. Why: Retention solves a different requirement.

Verification: Use an in-scope account to label test content and an excluded account to verify the intended publishing boundary.

Practical exercise: Draft a label specification with classification meaning, protection settings and publishing audience.

L34 — DLP requirements

Objective pointer: D2.P1.1. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: A DLP requirement connects a location, detection condition and supported action. One workload's action should not be assumed for all others.

Northstar Consulting scenario: Finance must prevent synthetic account details being emailed externally while allowing the approved internal workflow.

Recommended approach: Specify Exchange as the location, the relevant detection and external-recipient conditions, and a supported restrictive action with test cases.

  • Alternative to reject: Configure only endpoint USB control. Why: That does not address this outbound email requirement.
  • Alternative to reject: Write a rule without its workload location. Why: Available conditions and actions depend on the location.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume a sensitivity label alone covers every outgoing path. Why: Labeling and DLP enforcement are separate decisions.

Verification: Test matching external mail, permitted internal mail and benign external mail; inspect policy events and user experience.

Practical exercise: Write a six-location DLP requirements matrix for Exchange, SharePoint, OneDrive, Teams, endpoint and Copilot.

L35 — Copilot DLP boundaries

Objective pointer: D2.P1.1. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Copilot DLP must use supported scenario-specific conditions and actions; it does not automatically govern every agent interaction.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A team wants labelled documents excluded from Copilot processing while preserving separately authorized document access.

Recommended approach: Specify the supported Copilot DLP label condition and processing restriction, then test the exact experience and limitations.

  • Alternative to reject: Remove every user's document permission regardless of the requirement. Why: That changes access beyond the stated processing restriction.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume the rule blocks all agents in every platform. Why: Agent controls have separate workload boundaries.
  • Alternative to reject: Treat a citation as proof the document text was processed. Why: A citation can remain even when content processing is restricted.

Verification: Record the relevant policy scope, labelled test item and processing behavior; distinguish a citation from use of item content.

Practical exercise: Prepare separate requirements for labelled grounding, sensitive prompt handling and agent communication.

L36 — Retention design

Objective pointer: D2.P1.3. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Retention rules govern preservation and disposal over time. Labels can express item-specific needs within a broader location policy.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A site retains ordinary documents for three years; a particular record needs seven years from the same start date.

Recommended approach: Apply the appropriate item retention label and deployment policy while accounting for the broader retention requirements.

  • Alternative to reject: Use a sensitivity label to set seven-year preservation. Why: Sensitivity and retention labels serve different functions.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume a shorter delete action defeats active retention. Why: An active retention requirement prevents ordinary permanent deletion.
  • Alternative to reject: Treat backup as the approved records schedule. Why: Recovery and lifecycle requirements are separate.

Verification: Document the record's applicable retain actions, start event and expected earliest ordinary disposal time.

Practical exercise: Draw a timeline showing a three-year site retain action and seven-year item retain action.

L37 — DLP alert response

Objective pointer: D2.P1.4. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: A DLP alert needs context about policy, item, actor and action before a responder can judge exposure and tune controls.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A DLP alert identifies an attempted external sharing action, but its status does not show whether the action was blocked.

Recommended approach: Inspect the matched rule, workload, affected content and recorded enforcement outcome before classifying the event.

  • Alternative to reject: Assume every alert proves a successful data leak. Why: An attempted action may have been prevented.
  • Alternative to reject: Disable the policy after one complaint. Why: One event does not establish that the rule is wrong.
  • Alternative to reject: Treat the alert name as complete evidence. Why: The underlying event provides the decision context.

Verification: Record attempted versus completed activity, blocked versus allowed outcome and any justified follow-up.

Practical exercise: Write a triage note that separates confirmed exposure from a blocked attempt.

L38 — DSPM for AI

Objective pointer: D2.P1.5. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Data security posture management identifies and prioritizes data risks; findings need owners, remediation and later verification.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A posture assessment finds many sensitive documents exposed to broad internal groups before an AI rollout.

Recommended approach: Prioritize the high-impact exposures, assign remediation owners and verify corrected sharing and relevant controls.

  • Alternative to reject: Treat the dashboard as automatic remediation of every permission. Why: Visibility is not the same as a completed fix.
  • Alternative to reject: Ignore internal oversharing because no guest is involved. Why: Unnecessary internal access is still a data risk.
  • Alternative to reject: Measure success only by viewing the assessment. Why: An activity count does not prove reduced exposure.

Verification: Compare targeted before-and-after access findings and direct tests, noting assessment refresh timing.

Practical exercise: Create a five-item risk register with severity, owner, control, due date and verification.

Primary expansion reading:
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/protect-information-purview/
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/prevent-data-loss-lifecycle-purview/

M08 — Copilot readiness and rollout

UNDERSTAND FIRST · MODULE 08

Roll out Copilot only when the people and data are ready

In simple English

Copilot can help users work with information they are permitted to use. Grounding means supplying relevant information to help produce a response. A citation points to source material; you still need to check whether the answer represents that material correctly. A licence is only part of readiness. You also need supported access, applications, data and support arrangements.

How it works

Assess user readiness and data readiness separately. For users, check identity, entitlement, supported experience and operational support. For data, check whether important material is accurate, usable and shared with the right people. Broad existing access can make sensitive material easier to discover. Start with a small, defined group and known sources. Record the task, the expected source and the observed answer. Expand based on evidence instead of the number of licences purchased.

Worked example

Fictional example: 30 pilot users can open Copilot, but a finance folder is shared too widely. The technical access test passes; the data-readiness gate does not. Correct the sharing problem before widening exposure. If a permitted file is not cited, investigate source access and discovery/indexing before granting more access. An empty answer is not a reason to make the whole site public.

Use this process

  1. Choose a small pilot and a useful, measurable task.
  2. Check the users' identity, licence and client prerequisites.
  3. Review permissions and source quality for pilot content.
  4. Test permitted retrieval and a known denied boundary.
  5. Record quality, unresolved risks and support needs before expansion.

Keep this distinction: Purchased seats are not active use. Active use is not useful output. Useful output is not proof that every data boundary is safe.

Stop and answer: All pilot users are licensed, but a sensitive site is overshared. Is the rollout ready?

Check your reasoning

No. Correct or contain the data exposure and verify the intended access boundary before expanding the rollout.

Read with the mapped lessons below. Microsoft reference

Estimated study: 4 hours. Lab prerequisites: Readiness and usage-report access plus scoped Copilot administration; licensed pilot users for live experiences.

A useful rollout has a business hypothesis: which task should improve, for whom, by how much and with what quality threshold? Define the baseline before asking users whether the new tool is impressive. A faster draft with more checking work may not save time overall.

Readiness has a technical side and a data side. Supported clients and entitlements make the capability available; appropriate source access makes it suitable for use. Existing oversharing can become much more visible when retrieval is easier. Correct unnecessary access at the source and verify it under the affected identity.

A phased rollout lets you learn with a small population. Set exit criteria before the pilot: required controls, support readiness, acceptable output quality and evidence of useful use. The decision to expand should follow these results. A severe unresolved exposure is a blocker even if satisfaction is high. Review the result with both the service owner and the data owner.

Key concepts: Copilot readiness, Data readiness, User experiences, Phased rollout decisions.

Diagram to create: Draw a launch gate with technical readiness, data readiness, support readiness and task-quality evidence all feeding the decision.

Knowledge check with answer: Why is license assignment insufficient for launch? Answer: it proves neither supported experiences nor safe data access or useful outcomes.

Common mistakes: Calling interest adoption, assuming AI creates new permission grants, and averaging mandatory control failures into a good score.

Exam tip: Look for the missing prerequisite or evidence, not the most ambitious rollout action.

L39 — Copilot readiness

Objective pointer: D3.C1.1. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Readiness combines identity, entitlement, supported clients, service configuration and operational support. A purchased seat is only one component.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A pilot has licenses but mixed client versions and no support process for access failures.

Recommended approach: Assess the tenant and in-app prerequisites, correct readiness gaps and define pilot support before expansion.

  • Alternative to reject: Declare readiness from seat count alone. Why: Entitlement does not prove supported client or service configuration.
  • Alternative to reject: Roll out to everyone before testing. Why: That scales unresolved defects.
  • Alternative to reject: Use user enthusiasm as technical acceptance. Why: Interest does not validate readiness.

Verification: Maintain a pass, fail or untested checklist for prerequisites and complete a representative pilot task.

Practical exercise: Create a readiness scorecard with evidence owners and explicit launch blockers.

L40 — Data readiness

Objective pointer: D3.C1.2. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: AI can make already-accessible information easier to find; existing oversharing therefore matters before rollout.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A pilot answer surfaces old HR content that the user can already open through a broad group grant.

Recommended approach: Correct the unintended source access and review similar sensitive content before expanding the rollout.

  • Alternative to reject: Assume Copilot invented new permissions. Why: The observed grant already explains access.
  • Alternative to reject: Hide only one answer. Why: The underlying source exposure remains.
  • Alternative to reject: Give everyone access to make results consistent. Why: That widens the problem.

Verification: Prove the affected user's direct access changes as intended and then retest representative retrieval after propagation.

Practical exercise: Produce an oversharing remediation ticket with root grant, affected population and validation.

L41 — User experiences

Objective pointer: D3.C1.6. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Adoption support should target actual job tasks, supported experiences and user feedback while preserving controls.

Northstar Consulting scenario: Users have Copilot access but do not know how to use it for their approved meeting and document workflows.

Recommended approach: Provide role-specific task guidance and supported experience checks, then measure repeated useful use and feedback.

  • Alternative to reject: Grant extra tenant administration to all users. Why: Administrative rights are not training.
  • Alternative to reject: Report licenses assigned as successful adoption. Why: Availability does not establish use.
  • Alternative to reject: Remove data controls to make demonstrations impressive. Why: That sacrifices the rollout's requirements.

Verification: Review workload-level activity together with task completion and user-reported quality, not merely seat counts.

Practical exercise: Design a 15-minute onboarding exercise where users verify a generated summary against its source.

L42 — Phased rollout decisions

Objective pointer: D3.C1.1. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: A pilot is an experiment with acceptance criteria, not a ceremony before a predetermined launch.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A small rollout succeeds on speed but has unresolved restricted-data exposure and no tested support escalation.

Recommended approach: Hold expansion until the exposure and operational blockers meet the predefined acceptance criteria.

  • Alternative to reject: Expand because response speed improved. Why: A benefit does not cancel a mandatory control failure.
  • Alternative to reject: Average security failures into a passing satisfaction score. Why: Mandatory controls are not offset by unrelated successes.
  • Alternative to reject: Delete the negative feedback. Why: That removes evidence rather than resolving the issue.

Verification: Re-run the failed access and escalation scenarios and record approval against the same launch criteria.

Practical exercise: Write a pilot exit decision with benefit evidence, blockers, remediation owners and a retest date.

Primary expansion reading:
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/enable-manage-microsoft-365-copilot/

M09 — Copilot controls and integrations

UNDERSTAND FIRST · MODULE 09

Keep Copilot settings, providers and connectors separate

In simple English

Web search brings public-web information into supported Copilot responses. Internal search helps find work information. A connector brings an external source into supported search or retrieval. An AI provider supplies a model for eligible experiences. Cowork has its own access and spending controls. The right choice depends on the requirement; these are not interchangeable features.

How it works

First identify whether the request changes information sources, the model provider, user access or funding. For connectors, distinguish an indexed copy from live retrieval. Review identity mapping and source permissions. For providers, check the specific offering and data terms before enabling it. For Cowork, review spending-policy scope; the old agent entry is not the current access control. A web-search setting changes grounding behaviour, not Cowork entitlement.

Worked example

Fictional example: Northstar wants an approved external policy source available to staff. A connector may meet that need, but it must preserve the intended item boundary. Test a known permitted record and the same record as a denied identity. If the business instead wants a different model, review provider settings and terms. Do not enable a provider just to solve an indexing problem.

Use this process

  1. Write the requested change as source, provider, access, experience or funding.
  2. Identify the owning control and the specific product behaviour.
  3. Review source permissions or provider terms as appropriate.
  4. Pilot the supported change and allow documented propagation.
  5. Verify the boundary with the intended identity and retain unresolved findings.

Keep this distinction: No indexing does not mean no runtime data transfer. A small Cowork spending limit does not mean no access. Microsoft-subprocessor status alone does not settle every geographic processing question.

Stop and answer: A user must have no Cowork access. Is setting a small positive credit limit sufficient?

Check your reasoning

No. Review every applicable spending policy that selects Cowork and remove the user from access-granting scope as required.

Read with the mapped lessons below. Microsoft reference

Estimated study: 4 hours. Lab prerequisites: AI administration and relevant Microsoft 365 Apps Cloud Policy role; external-source permission for connector work; approved billing only if exercising metered services.

AI settings are distributed because they control different boundaries. The capability a user sees, the model a service can use, the source it can retrieve and the meter that pays for execution are separate. Build a control register rather than expecting one master switch.

Connectors extend the information available to an experience. An indexed connection brings content and access metadata into an index; a runtime retrieval model has different storage and permission checkpoints. Both require identity and data-flow analysis. A working connection is not enough: a permitted user must retrieve the expected record, while a user without source access must fail for the correct reason.

New features change quickly. Cowork is a practical example of why an old registry entry cannot be assumed to remain the access control. Check current product documentation, tenant availability and effective scope. For a training exercise, inspecting configuration can be sufficient; enabling a billing policy is a separate financial action and is not necessary to learn the distinction.

Key concepts: Copilot web search, Copilot Search, Tenant Copilot settings, Cowork administration, Providers and connectors.

Diagram to create: Draw separate lanes for experience visibility, access grant, model selection, data retrieval and billing. Add indexed versus runtime connector flows.

Knowledge check with answer: Why does no indexing not mean no data transfer? Answer: a runtime connector still retrieves information for the requested operation.

Common mistakes: Treating disclaimers as enforcement, hidden features as revoked access, tiny budgets as no access, and Ready status as verified isolation.

Exam tip: Read which boundary the requirement names. Choose the control for that boundary and verify its current implementation.

L43 — Copilot web search

Objective pointer: D3.C1.3. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Web grounding and work-data grounding have different information boundaries. Policy scope must be checked in the effective experience.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A pilot should retain permitted work-data use but restrict web grounding according to the organization's requirement.

Recommended approach: Configure the supported web-search policy for the intended users and modes, then verify effective scope and retrieval timing.

  • Alternative to reject: Disable all SharePoint access. Why: That disrupts internal work content rather than targeting web search.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume no citation proves web search is disabled. Why: A response might not have needed web search.
  • Alternative to reject: Use a billing limit as a web-search policy. Why: Funding and search behavior are separate controls.

Verification: Inspect the effective policy assignment and test relevant modes after policy retrieval, recording uncertainty where runtime output is inconclusive.

Practical exercise: Create a mode-by-mode policy worksheet for work, web and chat experiences.

L44 — Copilot Search

Objective pointer: D3.C1.4. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Search configuration influences finding permitted information; permission and source quality still govern useful results.

Northstar Consulting scenario: Staff cannot find approved material through Copilot Search even though they can open the source directly.

Recommended approach: Check supported search configuration, source availability and indexing or retrieval status while preserving permissions.

  • Alternative to reject: Make the entire source public. Why: That is not a justified search repair.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume direct access guarantees immediate search appearance. Why: Search discovery has additional dependencies.
  • Alternative to reject: Treat missing search results as proof of permission denial. Why: Access must be tested separately.

Verification: Compare a known permitted item and a known denied item, documenting source access and discovery independently.

Practical exercise: Write a search test matrix containing query, identity, expected source and observed result.

L45 — Tenant Copilot settings

Objective pointer: D3.C1.5. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: AI tenant settings need distinct decisions for purchasing, admin experiences, releases, disclaimers and generation features.

Northstar Consulting scenario: The company wants controlled self-service purchases and a disclaimer while piloting new image features with approved users.

Recommended approach: Document and configure each supported setting at its actual scope, with an owner and validation for the pilot.

  • Alternative to reject: Assume a disclaimer prevents data loss. Why: A notice is not an enforcement control.
  • Alternative to reject: Use one purchase toggle as every AI control. Why: Purchasing, feature behavior and information protection differ.
  • Alternative to reject: Set every release preference to broad rollout without evaluation. Why: That contradicts a controlled pilot.

Verification: Capture the effective settings and test the intended user experiences; note any setting that lacks the desired granularity.

Practical exercise: Build a settings register for self-service purchase, admin Copilot, releases, disclaimer, video and image generation.

L46 — Cowork administration

Objective pointer: D3.C1.7. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Cowork access, discovery, model availability and web-search behavior are separate controls. Current access administration must be checked against current documentation.

Northstar Consulting scenario: An administrator blocks the old Cowork agent entry but leaves a user in a spending policy selecting Cowork.

Recommended approach: Review current spending-policy access scope and remove the user from every applicable Cowork access-granting scope if access must be prevented.

  • Alternative to reject: Rely solely on the legacy registry entry. Why: The current documented access control has moved.
  • Alternative to reject: Set an extremely small positive credit limit and call it no access. Why: A small limit still permits some use.
  • Alternative to reject: Hide discovery and assume access is revoked. Why: Visibility does not equal authorization.

Verification: Review all applicable spending policies plus product prerequisites, and distinguish effective access from discoverability.

Practical exercise: Prepare a no-charge configuration review; do not enable usage merely to prove that the control exists.

L47 — Providers and connectors

Objective pointer: D3.C1.8;D3.C1.9. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: External AI and source integrations need explicit data-flow and identity analysis; connector models differ in indexing and query-time retrieval.

Northstar Consulting scenario: An external repository is connected for a pilot, but security has not checked identity mapping, data handling or provider terms.

Recommended approach: Review the provider's processing arrangement and connector model, then verify source permissions with allowed and denied users.

  • Alternative to reject: Assume no indexing means no runtime data transfer. Why: Federated retrieval still transfers requested information.
  • Alternative to reject: Map every external identity to an all-staff account. Why: That defeats isolation.
  • Alternative to reject: Accept connection status as sufficient data-security evidence. Why: A healthy connector can still have incorrect access mapping.

Verification: Trace a synthetic record through the documented flow and test both authorized retrieval and the source or indexed access boundary.

Practical exercise: Draw indexed and federated data flows; add a separate provider-review checklist for processing terms and permitted data.

https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/configure-copilot-settings-extensibility/

M10 — Agent identity and access

UNDERSTAND FIRST · MODULE 10

Treat an agent as an actor with its own identity

In simple English

An agent can perform work through tools and services. To govern it, you need to know which identity acts, what access it has and which person is accountable. Microsoft Entra Agent ID distinguishes a blueprint, its tenant principal, a runtime agent identity and an optional paired user account. These objects have different jobs; a familiar display name does not make them the same object.

How it works

The blueprint provides the authentication foundation. The child agent identity represents the running agent. A sponsor provides business accountability; a Microsoft Entra owner provides technical administration. Registry ownership is another layer. Keep these responsibilities current when staff move or leave. Apply supported access controls to the actual token subject. A policy aimed at agent identities does not automatically cover a paired agent user account or a human user involved in a separate flow.

Worked example

Fictional example: three finance agents come from one blueprint. The organisation wants supported agent Conditional Access to cover current and future child identities. Blueprint-level targeting addresses that scope. It does not mean an API-key connection is governed by Entra Conditional Access, and it does not silently include paired user accounts. Name each identity and authentication path in your design.

Use this process

  1. Identify the agent and each identity or credential path it uses.
  2. Record sponsor, technical owner and registry responsibility.
  3. Check permissions and access-review requirements.
  4. Select the policy subject and supported control deliberately.
  5. Verify fresh relevant sign-in evidence after policy propagation.

Keep this distinction: An agent identity cannot complete a human MFA challenge. Agent identity, paired user account and human user are separate policy subjects. Moving a registry owner does not automatically update every identity owner.

Stop and answer: An agent uses an API key to reach a resource. Will an Entra token policy necessarily control that call?

Check your reasoning

No. The API-key path bypasses Entra token authentication. That path needs controls at the relevant service and credential boundary.

Read with the mapped lessons below. Microsoft reference

Estimated study: 4 hours. Lab prerequisites: Appropriate Entra Agent ID governance and Conditional Access roles; feature-specific Agent 365 and Entra licensing.

An agent is not simply another employee account. Its identity object, blueprint relationship, optional user account and delegated user context can produce different policy subjects. Before applying a control, identify who requests the token and which resource is involved.

Ownership also has layers. A business sponsor answers whether the agent should do the work; a technical owner maintains its identity; registry ownership supports curation. Put these responsibilities in a table and verify the actual objects instead of assuming one owner field resolves every obligation.

Lifecycle controls connect people changes to agent changes. When a sponsor leaves, transfer accountability or retire the agent deliberately. For access policy, supported blueprint scope can address present and future child identities, but it does not automatically cover separate user accounts or a legacy identity retained during migration. Inspect fresh token-request evidence after propagation. A successful API call alone does not tell you which policy was evaluated.

Key concepts: Agent identity lifecycle, Agent access governance, Agent ownership layers, Agent Conditional Access.

Diagram to create: Draw sponsor and technical owner connected to a blueprint, two child identities and one distinct agent user account. Mark the policy subject for each request.

Knowledge check with answer: Why cannot an agent identity complete human MFA? Answer: the noninteractive subject requires controls supported for that identity type.

Common mistakes: Confusing a child with its blueprint, registry owner with sponsor, and identity-targeted policy with user-account coverage.

Exam tip: Identify the token subject before choosing a policy or predicting inheritance.

L48 — Agent identity lifecycle

Objective pointer: D3.I1.1. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: An agent identity needs accountable ownership from creation through retirement. Its sponsor's departure is an operational event.

Northstar Consulting scenario: The business sponsor leaves while an agent continues processing project information.

Recommended approach: Use the supported agent lifecycle workflow to transfer sponsorship or retire the agent according to the approved decision.

  • Alternative to reject: Leave the agent permanently unowned. Why: No accountable person remains for business decisions.
  • Alternative to reject: Copy the departed employee's credentials into the agent. Why: That creates an inappropriate identity dependency.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume deleting a registry description revokes all resource access. Why: Presentation and access lifecycle are different.

Verification: Inspect the resulting sponsor or retirement state and verify required access changes and audit evidence.

Practical exercise: Design a sponsor-departure workflow with transfer approval, failure handling and review date.

L49 — Agent access governance

Objective pointer: D3.I1.2. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: An agent's access should match its task and resource boundaries; supported access packages and policy controls need deliberate targeting.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A procurement agent needs temporary read access to a defined resource set for an approved pilot.

Recommended approach: Use the supported governed access arrangement for the agent and resource, with limited scope, ownership and an end-date review.

  • Alternative to reject: Give the agent every permission used by Global Administrator. Why: The scope exceeds the task.
  • Alternative to reject: Grant permanent access because the pilot is short. Why: Short duration strengthens the case for explicit expiry or removal.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume publishing the agent grants all required resource permissions. Why: Availability and resource authorization differ.

Verification: Validate a required operation and a prohibited operation under the actual agent identity, then confirm expiry or removal behavior.

Practical exercise: Write an access request specifying identity, resources, actions, business purpose and end condition.

L50 — Agent ownership layers

Objective pointer: D3.I1.3. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Business sponsorship, technical identity ownership and registry ownership need not be the same person or object.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A registry lists an owner, but the identity team finds no current business sponsor for the agent identity.

Recommended approach: Reconcile registry ownership, Entra technical ownership and business sponsorship as separate responsibilities.

  • Alternative to reject: Assume the registry field populates every ownership layer. Why: Ownership surfaces are not interchangeable.
  • Alternative to reject: Remove all owners to avoid duplication. Why: That removes accountability.
  • Alternative to reject: Treat a billing contact as automatic resource approver. Why: Billing and authorization roles are distinct.

Verification: Produce an ownership matrix tied to the relevant objects and verify that each accountable person accepts the responsibility.

Practical exercise: Draft a RACI for business approval, identity administration, registry curation and incident response.

L51 — Agent Conditional Access

Objective pointer: D3.I1.2. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Agent-token policy evaluation depends on the subject, resource and supported controls; agents cannot satisfy a human authentication prompt.

Northstar Consulting scenario: Current and future child identities from one agent blueprint require a supported blocking policy. An agent user account also exists.

Recommended approach: Use supported blueprint-level scope for child identities and assess the agent user account separately with its supported controls.

  • Alternative to reject: Target one child and assume future children are covered. Why: Individual identity scope does not cover new siblings.
  • Alternative to reject: Require the agent identity to perform interactive MFA. Why: The nonhuman subject cannot satisfy that human interaction.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume blueprint targeting automatically includes the agent user account. Why: The user account is a separate policy subject.

Verification: Use fresh token-request evidence for included and excluded identities after propagation, and assess the separate user-account policy.

Practical exercise: Draw blueprint, child identity and agent user account as distinct nodes with separate policy scopes.

Primary expansion reading:
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/establish-agent-identities-entra-agent-id/

M11 — Agent registry and tool governance

UNDERSTAND FIRST · MODULE 11

Review an agent before people can use its capabilities

In simple English

An agent registry helps administrators discover and manage agents. Publication, installation, availability and access are related but distinct decisions. A tool is a capability an agent can call. Approving an agent's name or description is not enough; you must understand its tools, permissions, data sources and intended audience.

How it works

For a new request, identify the agent's purpose, publisher, owner and users. Review required tools and data access. Decide whether it should be published, rejected or returned for changes. Use the registry's supported actions for the intended outcome. Then verify what the target users can actually do. A block or removal needs outcome evidence; do not assume a catalogue change removes every underlying credential or source permission.

Worked example

Fictional example: a draft agent should search an approved policy library. Its proposed tool can also modify records in a business system. The extra capability does not follow from the search requirement. Ask for a narrower design or reject the request pending correction. If the agent is later approved, test the allowed task and a disallowed operation. Keep tool permission and agent availability evidence separate.

Use this process

  1. Inventory purpose, publisher, owner, audience, tools and sources.
  2. Compare each permission with a required task.
  3. Record the publication or rejection decision and conditions.
  4. Scope installation and access using supported registry controls.
  5. Test effective availability and tool behaviour for the intended audience.

Keep this distinction: Discovery is inventory, not automatic approval. Publication is not blanket data permission. Removing a visible agent does not prove its separate credentials are revoked.

Stop and answer: A read-only assistant requests a write-capable tool. Should you approve it because the description says read-only?

Check your reasoning

No. Review and constrain actual capabilities. A description does not enforce the boundary.

Read with the mapped lessons below. Microsoft reference

Estimated study: 4 hours. Lab prerequisites: Appropriate agent registry curation and tool-management permissions; do not assume view access includes install or consent rights.

An agent catalog is a governed inventory, not a universal list of everything anyone could have built. Reconcile it against platforms and team reports. Record draft visibility and integration gaps as findings. An incomplete count should prompt investigation, not an assertion that missing agents cannot exist.

Publishing decisions need specific evidence: purpose, owners, permissions, data sources, tools and negative tests. Approving a useful purpose is not approving every requested capability. Return a request for changes when the privilege is broader than the business task.

Tools deserve their own review because they determine what the agent can do. Read-only retrieval, sending a message and changing a shared record have different consequences. A tool block and an agent block can contain different scopes. During response, choose the smallest effective containment supported by the evidence, but verify it actually stops the problematic operation.

Key concepts: Agent settings, Agent inventory, Agent publication review, Registry actions and tools.

Diagram to create: Draw request → review → approve, return or reject, then show agent access and tool access as separate gates.

Knowledge check with answer: Why is a registry owner not proof that the agent has safe tools? Answer: accountability and permitted capabilities are different properties.

Common mistakes: Equating installed with approved for everyone, registry inventory with all possible agents, or view permission with management permission.

Exam tip: Check whether the question targets tenant defaults, one agent, one user population or a tool.

L52 — Agent settings

Objective pointer: D3.I1.4. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Tenant agent settings shape permitted creation, sharing, templates and user availability; these are governance choices with different scopes.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A pilot allows an approved agent type to a limited group and prohibits broad creator sharing.

Recommended approach: Review allowed types, sharing, template and user-access settings, then test the actual pilot and excluded population.

  • Alternative to reject: Assume approval of one agent changes every tenant setting. Why: Instance decisions and tenant settings differ.
  • Alternative to reject: Publish all templates to everyone. Why: That exceeds the bounded pilot.
  • Alternative to reject: Use a sensitivity-label name as the sharing configuration. Why: Content labels do not substitute for agent settings.

Verification: Record effective settings and both permitted and prohibited sharing or access attempts.

Practical exercise: Prepare an agent-settings register with default, pilot exception, owner and expiry.

L53 — Agent inventory

Objective pointer: D3.I1.5. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: A registry enables discovery and curation, but inventory visibility depends on integration and discovery coverage.

Northstar Consulting scenario: The dashboard count is lower than the number of agents reported by teams using multiple platforms.

Recommended approach: Reconcile inventory coverage, connected platforms and draft visibility before declaring that unlisted agents do not exist.

  • Alternative to reject: Assume the dashboard is a complete inventory of every possible platform. Why: Coverage and draft visibility can vary.
  • Alternative to reject: Delete reported agents from the audit worksheet. Why: That conceals the gap.
  • Alternative to reject: Treat usage count as the same as catalog count. Why: Available agents and active agents are different populations.

Verification: Maintain a reconciled inventory noting source platform, registry state, owner and uncovered agents.

Practical exercise: Build a five-agent inventory with one draft, one inactive agent and one disconnected platform.

L54 — Agent publication review

Objective pointer: D3.I1.6. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Publication is a governance decision that should evaluate purpose, ownership, permissions, data flows and evidence.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A requested agent has a useful purpose but asks for broad mailbox write access without justification.

Recommended approach: Return or reject the request pending scoped permissions and adequate test evidence.

  • Alternative to reject: Publish first and review permissions later. Why: That exposes the requested privilege before review.
  • Alternative to reject: Approve because the description sounds useful. Why: A business benefit does not justify every permission.
  • Alternative to reject: Treat a familiar publisher as proof of fit. Why: The actual requested access still needs assessment.

Verification: Keep the review decision, required changes and verification evidence attached to the specific version or request.

Practical exercise: Complete a publication review rubric with approve, return for changes or reject outcomes.

L55 — Registry actions and tools

Objective pointer: D3.I1.7;D3.I1.8. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Agent availability and the tools it can invoke are related but distinct control surfaces.

Northstar Consulting scenario: An approved custom agent invokes an unwanted tool. The incident requires disabling that capability while retaining unrelated approved tools.

Recommended approach: Use the supported tool control for the unwanted tool and verify the agent's resulting behavior; assess agent blocking if needed for containment.

  • Alternative to reject: Unpublish an unrelated agent. Why: That does not address the tool in question.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume installing the agent makes every tool acceptable. Why: Tools require their own governance.
  • Alternative to reject: Delete only the tool's description. Why: Metadata removal does not prove blocked invocation.

Verification: Observe the blocked invocation and continued permitted behavior, with corresponding governance records.

Practical exercise: Design a custom-agent intake checklist and a separate tool review with publisher, permissions and block test.

Primary expansion reading:
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/curate-control-agents-registry-tools/

M12 — Agent security and compliance operations

UNDERSTAND FIRST · MODULE 12

Monitor what agents actually do

In simple English

Agent governance continues after publication. You need evidence of activity, permissions, data handling and ownership. A compliance gap is a requirement that is not yet met or not yet evidenced. A quiet dashboard can mean low activity, incomplete coverage or missing data. It is not automatic proof that the agent is safe.

How it works

Start with the expected behaviour: what task, which sources, which tools and which audience? Compare observed activity with that baseline. Investigate unusual volume, unexpected destinations or sensitive-data events in context. Check the scope of your monitoring before interpreting an absence of alerts. Assign a named owner to each gap, define corrective work and verify the outcome. Agent 365 supports the monitoring and governance work, but the administrator must still interpret the evidence.

Worked example

Fictional example: an approved agent usually reads a policy source. Activity suddenly includes another destination. Investigate the identity, tool, request and source permissions. Do not call it a breach solely because the destination is new, and do not dismiss it solely because the agent was approved last month. Compare the activity with the authorised purpose and apply the response process when evidence justifies it.

Use this process

  1. Define expected agent behaviour and required monitoring coverage.
  2. Review activity with identity, tool, resource and time context.
  3. Separate confirmed behaviour from missing or delayed evidence.
  4. Assign remediation, owner and due date for each gap.
  5. Retest the boundary and document the closure evidence.

Keep this distinction: Approval is a decision at a point in time. Monitoring tests continued behaviour. Compliance claims require evidence against a stated requirement.

Stop and answer: An agent has no alerts, but you have not checked monitoring coverage. Can you declare it compliant?

Check your reasoning

No. Establish coverage and assess the applicable requirements before drawing that conclusion.

Read with the mapped lessons below. Microsoft reference

Estimated study: 3 hours. Lab prerequisites: Agent 365 observation and appropriate security or compliance roles; connected data sources and audit availability for live evidence.

An approved agent can behave unexpectedly later. Workload changes, new data, tool modifications or loops can change its risk. Monitoring should compare actual behavior with expected business activity and associate each finding with an accountable owner.

Trace sensitive information through the complete action path. Being allowed to read a file does not automatically authorize transmitting it to another recipient. Identify the source, identity, tool, destination and enforcement point. Test the actual path with synthetic data. An instruction in the agent prompt can guide behavior but is weaker than enforced access and data controls.

Compliance work is evidence management. List each requirement, its implemented control, the test result and limitations. A stable agent with no errors may still lack a required owner review or retention arrangement. Mark untested controls honestly. Closure requires evidence of remediation and an agreed residual-risk decision, not merely a green dashboard.

Key concepts: Agent activity monitoring, Agent data protection, Agent compliance gaps.

Diagram to create: Draw source → agent → tool → destination, with identity and data-control checkpoints; add an evidence-to-requirement compliance table.

Knowledge check with answer: Why is a tenfold activity spike not proof of compromise? Answer: legitimate demand, retries or a loop can also explain it.

Common mistakes: Treating approval as permanent safety, prompt wording as enforcement and product availability as organizational compliance.

Exam tip: Follow the data and the identity; distinguish attempted, permitted, blocked and unknown outcomes.

L56 — Agent activity monitoring

Objective pointer: D3.G1.1. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Monitoring links an agent's identity and actions to expected behavior, owners and anomalies. Counts alone do not establish safety.

Northstar Consulting scenario: An agent's calls increase tenfold overnight, but the cause might be legitimate demand or a repeated loop.

Recommended approach: Correlate activity, affected tools, errors, timing and business context before choosing a targeted response.

  • Alternative to reject: Declare compromise solely from the count. Why: Volume is a signal requiring context.
  • Alternative to reject: Ignore the increase because the agent was previously approved. Why: Approval does not make later behavior safe.
  • Alternative to reject: Increase the budget immediately. Why: That may fund an unintended loop.

Verification: Document the activity pattern and root-cause evidence, plus the effect of any containment or correction.

Practical exercise: Analyze a synthetic hourly usage series with a repeated error loop and a legitimate batch period.

L57 — Agent data protection

Objective pointer: D3.G1.2. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Agent data protection must match the actual workload and action path; a broad AI label is not a universal enforcement guarantee.

Northstar Consulting scenario: An agent reads a permitted file but attempts to send its sensitive contents through a different communication path.

Recommended approach: Map the read and send paths and apply the supported identity, permission and data-protection controls to the actual operation.

  • Alternative to reject: Assume the file's permitted read automatically permits external sending. Why: Read and dissemination are separate decisions.
  • Alternative to reject: Assume Copilot DLP universally covers every agent channel. Why: Coverage is workload-bound.
  • Alternative to reject: Rely only on a prompt asking the agent to be careful. Why: An instruction is not a substitute for enforced controls.

Verification: Test a synthetic sensitive outbound operation and a benign permitted operation under the actual agent execution path.

Practical exercise: Draw data source, agent, tool and recipient boundaries and identify where enforcement is verified.

L58 — Agent compliance gaps

Objective pointer: D3.G1.3. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: A compliance finding is a gap between required controls and observed evidence, not merely an unfavorable dashboard color.

Northstar Consulting scenario: An agent handles regulated records but has no documented owner review or tested retention requirements.

Recommended approach: Record the specific gaps, assign accountable remediation and require evidence before closing them.

  • Alternative to reject: Close the finding because the agent has no recent errors. Why: Reliability does not prove compliance.
  • Alternative to reject: Mark every control compliant because the product is Microsoft. Why: Organizational configuration and use still matter.
  • Alternative to reject: Replace evidence with a general policy statement. Why: The implemented control must be demonstrated.

Verification: Link each requirement to a configured control, tested outcome, owner and unresolved limitation.

Practical exercise: Create a compliance matrix containing met, unmet and not-yet-tested states.

Primary expansion reading:
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/agent-365-monitor-manage/
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/agent-365-govern/

M13 — AI economics, adoption and service health

UNDERSTAND FIRST · MODULE 13

Separate adoption, business value, cost and reliability

In simple English

Adoption tells you whether people use a service. Business value tells you whether useful work improves. Cost tells you what resources or subscriptions are consumed. Service health tells you whether the service is working. A single chart rarely answers all four questions. Match the report to the decision you need to make.

How it works

Define the reporting period, the users or agents in scope and the measure before comparing reports. A licensed-user count is not the same as an active-user count. A usage event is not necessarily a billable event. Reconcile charges with their funding route and avoid counting the same cost twice. For value, use an agreed task measure such as time to produce an accepted draft, including review effort. For reliability, combine the incident evidence with the users' actual symptoms.

Worked example

Fictional teaching data: 80 people are licensed, 48 are active in the chosen period, and 36 complete the target workflow. Active adoption among licensed users is 48/80 = 60%. Workflow completion among licensed users is 36/80 = 45%. These are different measures. If the workflow's review burden rises, higher activity alone does not establish better productivity. State the denominator instead of calling every percentage adoption.

Use this process

  1. Write the business question before opening a dashboard.
  2. Match the report's period, denominator and scope.
  3. Reconcile costs by service and funding route.
  4. Compare usage with task outcomes and support burden.
  5. Use service health and workload evidence to investigate reliability.

Keep this distinction: Seats are capacity. Active users show use. Accepted outcomes show one form of value. Charges require billing evidence. None of these should silently stand in for another.

Stop and answer: An activity report and a billing report show different totals. Is one necessarily wrong?

Check your reasoning

No. Check metric definitions, periods, processing delay and charge coverage. They may count different things.

Read with the mapped lessons below. Microsoft reference

Estimated study: 3 hours. Lab prerequisites: Billing or cost read access, usage-report access and Service health read access appropriate to the task.

Separate availability, activity and outcomes. Licenses show that people could use a capability. Active-user measures show defined behavior during a period. Neither alone proves a valuable business result. Pair activity with an accepted-task measure such as a source-checked summary meeting a quality rubric.

Cost needs careful boundaries. If two dashboards display the same metered charge, adding them inflates the total. Reconcile identifiers, periods and units. Keep distinct fixed and variable charges visible, and record what is excluded. Cost per accepted outcome is total relevant cost divided by outcomes meeting the agreed standard; it differs from cost per prompt.

A service-health problem can reduce usage, but low usage can also result from unsuitable tasks, training gaps or access defects. Investigate before attributing cause. An operations report should state what is known, what is inferred and what action will test the inference. This makes the report useful to both finance and the service owner.

Key concepts: AI cost reconciliation, Adoption and AI service health.

Diagram to create: Draw three columns: entitlement, activity and accepted outcomes. Add a cost-reconciliation table with overlap removal.

Knowledge check with answer: What is adoption for 40 active users among 100 licensed users? Answer: 40 percent for that defined population and period; it is not a value claim.

Common mistakes: Double-counting cost surfaces, comparing unequal report windows and equating prompts with productivity.

Exam tip: Write the denominator and period before calculating. Check whether the requested metric is usage, cost, value or health.

L59 — AI cost reconciliation

Objective pointer: D3.M1.1. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Cost reporting needs consistent periods, billing boundaries and units; overlapping views must not be added as separate charges.

Northstar Consulting scenario: A Microsoft 365 AI report shows 1200 currency units, including 300 also shown in an Azure view for the same metered use.

Recommended approach: Reconcile the common charge identifiers and period, then count the overlapping 300 only once.

  • Alternative to reject: Add both totals as independent charges. Why: That double-counts the same metered use.
  • Alternative to reject: Ignore all Azure costs. Why: Some separate charges may genuinely exist.
  • Alternative to reject: Report prompt count as currency. Why: Usage and financial measures require a defined conversion.

Verification: Produce a reconciled ledger with unique charges, overlaps, excluded taxes or credits and the reporting period.

Practical exercise: Reconcile 900 fixed cost, 300 shared metered cost and 100 additional distinct cost to a total of 1300.

L60 — Adoption and AI service health

Objective pointer: D3.M1.2;D3.M1.3;D3.M1.4. Outcome: given a requirement, select the appropriate control, reject the alternatives and specify evidence of the result.

Theory: Usage, useful outcomes and service reliability are different measures. Healthy adoption reporting defines the population and time window.

Northstar Consulting scenario: There are 100 licensed users, 40 active users and a suspected service incident. Leadership asks whether the rollout is successful.

Recommended approach: Report the defined 40 percent active-license rate, investigate service health separately and collect task-quality evidence before claiming value.

  • Alternative to reject: Call 100 purchased seats 100 percent adoption. Why: Licensing is not activity.
  • Alternative to reject: Treat a healthy service dashboard as proof users gain value. Why: Availability is not business benefit.
  • Alternative to reject: Infer a service outage solely from low usage. Why: Other causes such as training and workload fit remain possible.

Verification: Reconcile the report population and period, compare workload-level use and inspect matching service-health evidence.

Practical exercise: Write a dashboard commentary with adoption, cost per accepted outcome, incident status and next action.

Primary expansion reading:
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/training/modules/monitor-optimize-m365-ai-services/

Lab guide — 13 bounded exercises

Each lab uses the shared evidence standard above. Complete lessons before the lab; use the supplied CSV fixtures when a required tenant feature is missing. Numbered steps are a workflow, not a promise that portal labels remain unchanged. Current linked product guidance governs UI-specific implementation.

LAB-01 — Tenant foundations and licensing

Prerequisites and access: Tenant or Entra settings administrator; license administrator for entitlement work. Use a separate test tenant.

  • 1. Inventory a test tenant without changing production.
  • 2. Record its tenant identifier and approved admin role.
  • 3. Open organization settings and record one branding and one profile setting.
  • 4. Open domain settings and document verification status; use an offline DNS ticket if you do not own a spare domain.
  • 5. Inspect a pilot licensing group and distinguish direct from nested membership.
  • 6. Compare two test users' assigned products and service plans.
  • 7. Identify one missing entitlement in the supplied fixture.
  • 8. Draft a targeted correction rather than assigning a broader product automatically.
  • 9. Verify expected user coverage.
  • 10. Restore any temporary branding after capturing the result..

Expected deliverable and acceptance: Tenant baseline, DNS change ticket and entitlement reconciliation. Pass when the trainee separates verification from routing and detects the nested-membership gap.

Troubleshooting: if the feature is absent, check role, subscription and rollout status using the linked source. If a saved setting appears ineffective, verify target identity and scope, then documented propagation. If access changes unexpectedly, restore the recorded baseline for the disposable lab scope and stop expanding the change.

Cleanup evidence: record objects removed, settings restored and any retained artifact or unresolved dependency. Never delete a protected record just to obtain a clean lab.

LAB-02 — Operational resilience and funding

Prerequisites and access: Read roles for health and billing review; supported Backup role and approved billing for an optional live restore.

  • 1. Read Service health and record a current or historical advisory without assuming it affects the pilot.
  • 2. Write a notification plan rather than subscribing unrelated people.
  • 3. Compare the two synthetic connectivity records.
  • 4. Identify the branch-only failing path.
  • 5. Inspect Backup availability and licensing without enabling billable protection.
  • 6. In a funded sandbox only, protect one synthetic object and follow the supported restore workflow.
  • 7. Record target time, destination, job state and restored-content validation.
  • 8. For the offline route, choose the correct restore point from the fixture and explain uncertainty.
  • 9. Reconcile the AI-cost exercise.
  • 10. Remove only disposable lab objects and any lab-only billing configuration after review..

Expected deliverable and acceptance: Incident triage worksheet, restore acceptance record and funding matrix. Offline completion is a design exercise, not evidence of a live restore.

Troubleshooting: if the feature is absent, check role, subscription and rollout status using the linked source. If a saved setting appears ineffective, verify target identity and scope, then documented propagation. If access changes unexpectedly, restore the recorded baseline for the disposable lab scope and stop expanding the change.

Cleanup evidence: record objects removed, settings restored and any retained artifact or unresolved dependency. Never delete a protected record just to obtain a clean lab.

LAB-03 — Collaboration and safe discovery

Prerequisites and access: Exchange, Teams and SharePoint scoped administrators; supported Copilot and SharePoint Advanced Management entitlement for advanced live tests.

  • 1. Create or use a disposable collaboration workspace.
  • 2. Assign two pilot identities: permitted and excluded.
  • 3. On a test shared mailbox, document read and send delegation separately.
  • 4. Send only to a lab recipient if testing delivery.
  • 5. Create a team and inspect a standard and private channel membership boundary.
  • 6. Upload a synthetic finance document to a test site.
  • 7. Record its effective permission grants.
  • 8. Test its direct URL as both users.
  • 9. Where licensed, evaluate the supported discovery restriction and allow documented propagation; do not conclude failure from an immediate query.
  • 10. In a licensed lab meeting, review policy and organizer options, start a transcript with permission and validate the saved result.
  • 11. Restore pilot settings and delete disposable content subject to any retention controls..

Expected deliverable and acceptance: Permission matrix, two-identity access tests and meeting-state worksheet. Pass requires distinguishing discovery, authorization and transcript state.

Troubleshooting: if the feature is absent, check role, subscription and rollout status using the linked source. If a saved setting appears ineffective, verify target identity and scope, then documented propagation. If access changes unexpectedly, restore the recorded baseline for the disposable lab scope and stop expanding the change.

Cleanup evidence: record objects removed, settings restored and any retained artifact or unresolved dependency. Never delete a protected record just to obtain a clean lab.

LAB-04 — Identity administration at scale

Prerequisites and access: Supported User, Groups or Exchange roles for the task; Privileged Role Administrator for PIM configuration where authorized; read-only Graph permissions for inventory.

  • 1. Create a target inventory from the supplied identities fixture.
  • 2. Mark members, guests and contacts.
  • 3. Identify the duplicate proposed user principal name.
  • 4. Assign business owners to the guest review and leaver transfer.
  • 5. In a lab tenant, inspect a supported administrative-unit role assignment and compare in-scope and out-of-scope test objects.
  • 6. If PIM is available, inspect an eligible test role and its activation requirements; do not elevate a production account.
  • 7. Run the read-only Graph script after approved consent and tenant confirmation.
  • 8. Reconcile the export with the expected inventory.
  • 9. Draft a five-user write pilot plan without executing bulk writes.
  • 10. Disconnect the Graph session and retain sanitized evidence..

Expected deliverable and acceptance: Lifecycle checklist, delegated-scope test and read-only inventory. Pass requires explaining all exported fields and identifying the duplicate before any write.

Troubleshooting: if the feature is absent, check role, subscription and rollout status using the linked source. If a saved setting appears ineffective, verify target identity and scope, then documented propagation. If access changes unexpectedly, restore the recorded baseline for the disposable lab scope and stop expanding the change.

Cleanup evidence: record objects removed, settings restored and any retained artifact or unresolved dependency. Never delete a protected record just to obtain a clean lab.

LAB-05 — Authentication and access decisions

Prerequisites and access: Authentication Administrator or appropriate policy role; Conditional Access Administrator for a dedicated sandbox; required Entra licensing.

  • 1. Use the synthetic sign-in fixture before touching any tenant policy.
  • 2. Predict each user's outcome.
  • 3. Open sign-in logs in a permitted lab and locate one controlled test event.
  • 4. Record identity, resource, client, authentication and policy results.
  • 5. Review method availability and registration for a pilot user.
  • 6. Inspect SSPR scope and required methods.
  • 7. Draft a Conditional Access policy scoped only to test users, with a documented recovery route.
  • 8. Where authorized and licensed, create it report-only and inspect an included and excluded event.
  • 9. Do not convert it to enforcement merely to finish this lab.
  • 10. Export sanitized findings and remove the disposable report-only policy after review..

Expected deliverable and acceptance: Policy truth table and event-based explanation. Fail if the learner claims that report-only blocked a user or that one grant overrides a block.

Troubleshooting: if the feature is absent, check role, subscription and rollout status using the linked source. If a saved setting appears ineffective, verify target identity and scope, then documented propagation. If access changes unexpectedly, restore the recorded baseline for the disposable lab scope and stop expanding the change.

Cleanup evidence: record objects removed, settings restored and any retained artifact or unresolved dependency. Never delete a protected record just to obtain a clean lab.

LAB-06 — Threat protection and response

Prerequisites and access: Appropriate Defender security read or response role; Defender for Office 365 Plan 2 or equivalent entitlement for advanced investigation and simulation features.

  • 1. Read the synthetic incident timeline.
  • 2. Identify the delivered message and affected recipients.

  • 4. In a permitted Defender environment, inspect an example alert and its available linked evidence without executing remediation on real users.
  • 5. Review a test recipient's policy source for the relevant feature.
  • 6. Draft the targeted response and success criteria.
  • 7. Prepare a simulation plan for fictional lab recipients only.
  • 8. Do not launch a campaign from this workbook.
  • 9. Write an incident closure note naming completed and outstanding actions.
  • 10. Have a peer challenge one unsupported inference..

Expected deliverable and acceptance: Investigation timeline, policy-source analysis and simulation design. Live remediation or campaign delivery is not required for completion.

Troubleshooting: if the feature is absent, check role, subscription and rollout status using the linked source. If a saved setting appears ineffective, verify target identity and scope, then documented propagation. Read-only Graph PowerShell lab

Prerequisites: supported PowerShell, approved Microsoft Graph modules and consent for the read scope. Use a lab tenant, replace the tenant placeholder and review the scope before authenticating. User.Read.All is appropriate here for requested user properties; it is not claimed to be the least scope for every possible user query. This script reads and exports; it does not create, update or delete directory objects. The code is documentation-based and has not been executed against your tenant.

# If required, install approved modules using your organization’s normal process.
# Install-Module Microsoft.Graph.Authentication -Scope CurrentUser
# Install-Module Microsoft.Graph.Users -Scope CurrentUser
$LabTenantId = "REPLACE-WITH-AUTHORIZED-LAB-TENANT-ID"
Connect-MgGraph -TenantId $LabTenantId -Scopes "User.Read.All" -ContextScope Process
Get-MgContext | Select-Object TenantId, Account, Scopes
# STOP if the tenant or account is not the approved lab identity.
Get-MgUser -All -Property Id,DisplayName,UserPrincipalName,UserType,AccountEnabled |
    Select-Object Id,DisplayName,UserPrincipalName,UserType,AccountEnabled |
    Export-Csv -Path "./AB650-Lab-Users.csv" -NoTypeInformation -Encoding UTF8
Disconnect-MgGraph

Explain: Connect-MgGraph establishes the scoped session; Get-MgContext checks identity and tenant; Get-MgUser reads directory users; -All handles all returned pages; -Property requests selected properties; Select-Object controls output columns; Export-Csv creates local evidence; Disconnect-MgGraph ends the session. Inspect the CSV and compare record counts with a trusted reference. Store exports appropriately because real directory identifiers are personal or organizational data.

Source: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/powershell/microsoftgraph/authentication-commands
Source: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/powershell/module/microsoft.graph.users/get-mguser

Objective-to-lesson traceability

The identifiers below are LOCAL course pointers, not Microsoft-assigned objective codes. They locate the numbered bullet under each skills subsection in the current official study guide. For example, D1.T1.4 means domain 1, tenant subsection, bullet 4. W1 denotes workloads; D2 I1 identities, A1 authentication, S1 threat security, P1 Purview; D3 C1 Copilot, I1 agent implementation, G1 agent governance, M1 monitoring. Compound pointers mean one lesson covers more than one bullet. This preserves precise traceability without reproducing the blueprint. Foundation lessons are explicitly prerequisites; capstones and revision inherit the mapped lessons.
