Integrated enterprise case studies

Case A — Acquisition launch

Scenario: Northstar adds a 30-person division. Twenty users are direct members of a licensed group; ten are in a nested group. A verified domain must keep its old MX route until Friday. Finance has a broadly shared library and a shared support mailbox whose delegate cannot send as support.

Mapping: L01–L13; D1 and D3.C1.2

Task: Produce a launch checklist, identify three independent control failures, choose targeted corrections and prove continuity.

Model answer and rationale: Expected: preserve mail routing until cutover; repair direct license coverage; trace and remove unnecessary finance access; configure the specific mailbox send permission. Validate users, DNS and direct access separately. Reject changing MX early, assuming nested inheritance or using search exclusion as the complete access repair.

Case B — Identity and phishing incident

Scenario: A phishing message reaches six people. One clicks. A support administrator has a permanently active broad role. A risky sign-in coincides with the click, but compromise is unconfirmed. A second applicable policy blocks the resource even after MFA.

Mapping: L14–L31; D2.I1, D2.A1, D2.S1

Task: Write a time-ordered evidence table, targeted response, access prediction and privileged-role improvement.

Model answer and rationale: Expected: distinguish delivery, click and compromise; correlate sign-in evidence; predict the applicable block; record remediation outcomes; use suitable least privilege and time-bound activation. Do not infer six compromised devices or that MFA cancels blocking.

Case C — Sensitive records and AI pilot

Scenario: The pilot retrieves HR content via an unintended group grant. A regulated record requires seven years of preservation from creation, while the site requires three. A blocked DLP attempt is reported as a confirmed leak.

Mapping: L32–L42; D2.P1 and D3.C1

Task: Create a data remediation plan, retention timeline and corrected incident statement; decide whether to expand the pilot.

Model answer and rationale: Expected: fix source access; apply appropriate item retention and distribution; preserve the longer active retain requirement; distinguish prevented attempt from successful exposure; hold expansion until required controls pass. Do not trade a mandatory data control for high satisfaction.

Case D — Agent estate and economics

Scenario: A sponsor leaves. An agent blueprint has two current children and another planned. A separate user account exists. A registry tool is unexpectedly used. Costs show an overlapping 300 charge; 40 of 100 licensed users are active.

Mapping: L43–L60; D3

Task: Produce an identity diagram, lifecycle decision, tool containment, reconciled cost model and one-page operational review.

Model answer and rationale: Expected: transfer or retire sponsorship; distinguish identity, user account and registry ownership; use supported scope for future children and separately assess user-account policies; block the relevant capability and verify; remove duplicate charges; state 40% activity for the defined period without claiming proven ROI.

Case scoring: 20 points each: 4 for identifying the real requirement and scope, 4 for the appropriate control, 4 for rejecting tempting alternatives, 4 for positive and negative evidence, and 4 for rollback, ownership and limitations. Pass gate: at least 16/20 and no critical error such as knowingly broadening sensitive access, confusing report-only with enforcement or claiming untested behavior as verified.

Instructor and facilitator guide

Delivery options: independent reading with self-marking; 6–10 minute narrated micro-lessons recorded from the outline; or four optional 60-minute workshops. These are delivery designs, not claims of affiliation with Microsoft Learn, CBT Nuggets or Udemy. Use the original examples and your own recording assets.

Before delivery: confirm the blueprint and high-change feature pages; verify the training tenant and license matrix; preload synthetic fixtures; test demonstration identities; keep a fallback offline worksheet; separate learner and administrator accounts; prepare captions and readable screenshots. Do not promise attendees a free tenant or access to every feature.

WorkshopPreparation60-minute agendaExpected evidence
1: tenant and collaborationM01–M0310 retrieval; 15 demo; 20 paired diagnosis; 10 review; 5 next stepsCase A launch record
2: identity and threat responseM04–M0610 retrieval; 15 log reading; 20 incident exercise; 10 challenge; 5 debriefCase B evidence timeline
3: data and Copilot rolloutM07–M0910 retrieval; 15 control comparison; 20 gate review; 10 feedback; 5 actionsCase C go/hold decision
4: agent operationsM10–M1310 retrieval; 15 identity diagram; 20 operations review; 10 challenge; 5 exam planCase D operating model

Repeatable facilitation method: ask learners to predict before demonstrating; show one successful and one denied operation; ask which observation could falsify their explanation; reveal the model answer after discussion. If learners choose a broad role, ask for the exact operation and object scope. If they quote a dashboard, ask for the underlying event. Avoid turning the exercise into memorizing portal clicks.

Assess each module using its knowledge check, associated five-question lesson sets and lab rubric. Give feedback in requirement → choice → evidence → limitation order. Advanced extension: introduce a conflicting control, stale token, unavailable entitlement or disconnected source and ask what additional evidence is needed. Do not give an advanced learner permission to skip the negative test.

Quality assurance for a training provider: pilot the bank with independent learners, inspect item-level errors and ambiguity, recheck product facts and revise before making any validated-readiness claim. Track first-attempt exam results separately from course completion and repeated practice. No cohort outcome data exists for this package; its mastery thresholds are course design choices.

Slide deck outline and recording plan

Each module has eight proposed slides. This is the requested outline, not a completed branded slide deck. Record the theory segment and demonstration separately so volatile UI footage can be replaced. Add alt text to diagrams and captions to recordings.

Deck segment M01 — Tenant foundations and licensing

  • Slide 01.1: Business problem — use the first lesson scenario; ask for an initial prediction.
  • Slide 01.2: Concepts — explain Branding, Domain verification, Organization settings, Group licensing.
  • Slide 01.3: Diagram — Create a two-layer tenant map: identity and administration above workload resources. Mark domain verification separately from mail-routing arrows.
  • Slide 01.4: Decision method — compare the correct control with the lesson distractors.
  • Slide 01.5: Demonstration — use LAB-01; show baseline, scope and result.
  • Slide 01.6: Failure investigation — Confusing appearance with trust, a group assignment with every user entitlement, and a saved value with effective behavior.
  • Slide 01.7: Retrieval check — Why can a verified domain still deliver mail elsewhere? Answer: verification proves ownership; routing follows the configured mail records.
  • Slide 01.8: Practice and transfer — submit Tenant baseline, DNS change ticket and entitlement reconciliation. Pass when the trainee separates verification from routing and detects the nested-membership gap.

Deck segment M02 — Operational resilience and funding

  • Slide 02.1: Business problem — use the first lesson scenario; ask for an initial prediction.
  • Slide 02.2: Concepts — explain AI entitlements and funding, Backup and recovery, Network diagnosis, Service health.
  • Slide 02.3: Diagram — Draw a fault tree with client, identity, network and cloud-service branches. Add a separate recovery timeline with incident time and chosen restore point.
  • Slide 02.4: Decision method — compare the correct control with the lesson distractors.
  • Slide 02.5: Demonstration — use LAB-02; show baseline, scope and result.
  • Slide 02.6: Failure investigation — Confusing funding with permission, advisory scope with every failure, and retention with recovery.
  • Slide 02.7: Retrieval check — Why can a successful restore job still fail business acceptance? Answer: it may restore the wrong point, wrong scope or incomplete expected content.
  • Slide 02.8: Practice and transfer — submit Incident triage worksheet, restore acceptance record and funding matrix. Offline completion is a design exercise, not evidence of a live restore.

Deck segment M03 — Collaboration and safe discovery

  • Slide 03.1: Business problem — use the first lesson scenario; ask for an initial prediction.
  • Slide 03.2: Concepts — explain Shared mailbox permissions, Teams membership, Meeting Copilot and transcripts, SharePoint permissions, Discovery versus access.
  • Slide 03.3: Diagram — Draw user → permission → resource, with a separate discovery branch. Make a table comparing mailbox read and send permissions.
  • Slide 03.4: Decision method — compare the correct control with the lesson distractors.
  • Slide 03.5: Demonstration — use LAB-03; show baseline, scope and result.
  • Slide 03.6: Failure investigation — Assuming channel membership is universal, Full Access includes Send As, or transcription allowed means a saved transcript exists.
  • Slide 03.7: Retrieval check — Can hiding a site in broad search remove a saved link permission? Answer: no; test and repair the underlying access separately.
  • Slide 03.8: Practice and transfer — submit Permission matrix, two-identity access tests and meeting-state worksheet. Pass requires distinguishing discovery, authorization and transcript state.

Deck segment M04 — Identity administration at scale

  • Slide 04.1: Business problem — use the first lesson scenario; ask for an initial prediction.
  • Slide 04.2: Concepts — explain User lifecycle, Guests and external collaboration, Group purpose, Privileged roles and PIM, Administrative units, Contacts, Graph PowerShell bulk administration.
  • Slide 04.3: Diagram — Draw joiner → mover → leaver with access and ownership checks at each transition; add capability, scope and time as three role dimensions.
  • Slide 04.4: Decision method — compare the correct control with the lesson distractors.
  • Slide 04.5: Demonstration — use LAB-04; show baseline, scope and result.
  • Slide 04.6: Failure investigation — Equating contacts with sign-in users, confusing role eligibility with activation, and treating administrative units as separate tenants.
  • Slide 04.7: Retrieval check — Why is a successful Graph connection not proof that 200 updates succeeded? Answer: it validates a session, not each target operation.
  • Slide 04.8: Practice and transfer — submit Lifecycle checklist, delegated-scope test and read-only inventory. Pass requires explaining all exported fields and identifying the duplicate before any write.

Deck segment M05 — Authentication and access decisions

  • Slide 05.1: Business problem — use the first lesson scenario; ask for an initial prediction.
  • Slide 05.2: Concepts — explain Authentication methods, Password Protection, Self-service password reset, Sign-in investigation, Conditional Access interaction, Risk-based access.
  • Slide 05.3: Diagram — Draw a policy decision tree: in scope? conditions match? report-only or enforced? applicable block? required controls satisfied?
  • Slide 05.4: Decision method — compare the correct control with the lesson distractors.
  • Slide 05.5: Demonstration — use LAB-05; show baseline, scope and result.
  • Slide 05.6: Failure investigation — Treating enablement as registration, no prompt as no MFA, or the newest Conditional Access policy as the winner.
  • Slide 05.7: Retrieval check — What does report-only failure establish? Answer: the policy evaluation indicates a failure under its logic, but report-only did not enforce blocking.
  • Slide 05.8: Practice and transfer — submit Policy truth table and event-based explanation. Fail if the learner claims that report-only blocked a user or that one grant overrides a block.

Deck segment M06 — Threat protection and response

  • Slide 06.1: Business problem — use the first lesson scenario; ask for an initial prediction.
  • Slide 06.2: Concepts — explain Alert triage, Threat policy scope, Threat policy precedence, Threat investigation and response, Attack simulation training.
  • Slide 06.3: Diagram — Draw an incident evidence chain from alert to message to affected identity to remediation result. Add a separate per-feature policy ladder.
  • Slide 06.4: Decision method — compare the correct control with the lesson distractors.
  • Slide 06.5: Demonstration — use LAB-06; show baseline, scope and result.
  • Slide 06.6: Failure investigation — Assuming custom priority zero beats a preset, every recipient was compromised, or simulation delivery proves learning.
  • Slide 06.7: Retrieval check — Why does closing an alert not prove containment? Answer: status may change without removing the message or addressing compromised access.
  • Slide 06.8: Practice and transfer — submit Investigation timeline, policy-source analysis and simulation design. Live remediation or campaign delivery is not required for completion.

Deck segment M07 — Information protection and governance

  • Slide 07.1: Business problem — use the first lesson scenario; ask for an initial prediction.
  • Slide 07.2: Concepts — explain Classification techniques, Sensitivity labels and publishing, DLP requirements, Copilot DLP boundaries, Retention design, DLP alert response, DSPM for AI.
  • Slide 07.3: Diagram — Draw five labeled boxes: classify, protect, restrict action, preserve, recover. Connect each to a different business requirement.
  • Slide 07.4: Decision method — compare the correct control with the lesson distractors.
  • Slide 07.5: Demonstration — use LAB-07; show baseline, scope and result.
  • Slide 07.6: Failure investigation — Confusing sensitivity with retention, label creation with publication, a citation with processing, and posture visibility with remediation.
  • Slide 07.7: Retrieval check — Why is a DLP alert not automatically proof of a leak? Answer: it may describe a prevented attempt; inspect the action outcome.
  • Slide 07.8: Practice and transfer — submit Classification rationale, six-location DLP matrix, alert triage and retention timeline. Pass requires separate allowed and denied test cases.

Deck segment M08 — Copilot readiness and rollout

  • Slide 08.1: Business problem — use the first lesson scenario; ask for an initial prediction.
  • Slide 08.2: Concepts — explain Copilot readiness, Data readiness, User experiences, Phased rollout decisions.
  • Slide 08.3: Diagram — Draw a launch gate with technical readiness, data readiness, support readiness and task-quality evidence all feeding the decision.
  • Slide 08.4: Decision method — compare the correct control with the lesson distractors.
  • Slide 08.5: Demonstration — use LAB-08; show baseline, scope and result.
  • Slide 08.6: Failure investigation — Calling interest adoption, assuming AI creates new permission grants, and averaging mandatory control failures into a good score.
  • Slide 08.7: Retrieval check — Why is license assignment insufficient for launch? Answer: it proves neither supported experiences nor safe data access or useful outcomes.
  • Slide 08.8: Practice and transfer — submit Readiness register, task-quality evaluation and launch decision. A useful answer must show the source checking performed by the learner.

Deck segment M09 — Copilot controls and integrations

  • Slide 09.1: Business problem — use the first lesson scenario; ask for an initial prediction.
  • Slide 09.2: Concepts — explain Copilot web search, Copilot Search, Tenant Copilot settings, Cowork administration, Providers and connectors.
  • Slide 09.3: Diagram — Draw separate lanes for experience visibility, access grant, model selection, data retrieval and billing. Add indexed versus runtime connector flows.
  • Slide 09.4: Decision method — compare the correct control with the lesson distractors.
  • Slide 09.5: Demonstration — use LAB-09; show baseline, scope and result.
  • Slide 09.6: Failure investigation — Treating disclaimers as enforcement, hidden features as revoked access, tiny budgets as no access, and Ready status as verified isolation.
  • Slide 09.7: Retrieval check — Why does no indexing not mean no data transfer? Answer: a runtime connector still retrieves information for the requested operation.
  • Slide 09.8: Practice and transfer — submit Control register, provider review and connector access-test record. Do not claim a live integration was tested when only the fixture was used.

Deck segment M10 — Agent identity and access

  • Slide 10.1: Business problem — use the first lesson scenario; ask for an initial prediction.
  • Slide 10.2: Concepts — explain Agent identity lifecycle, Agent access governance, Agent ownership layers, Agent Conditional Access.
  • Slide 10.3: Diagram — Draw sponsor and technical owner connected to a blueprint, two child identities and one distinct agent user account. Mark the policy subject for each request.
  • Slide 10.4: Decision method — compare the correct control with the lesson distractors.
  • Slide 10.5: Demonstration — use LAB-10; show baseline, scope and result.
  • Slide 10.6: Failure investigation — Confusing a child with its blueprint, registry owner with sponsor, and identity-targeted policy with user-account coverage.
  • Slide 10.7: Retrieval check — Why cannot an agent identity complete human MFA? Answer: the noninteractive subject requires controls supported for that identity type.
  • Slide 10.8: Practice and transfer — submit Identity diagram, ownership matrix, lifecycle workflow and scoped policy evidence. Pass requires explicitly separating agent identities from agent user accounts.

Deck segment M11 — Agent registry and tool governance

  • Slide 11.1: Business problem — use the first lesson scenario; ask for an initial prediction.
  • Slide 11.2: Concepts — explain Agent settings, Agent inventory, Agent publication review, Registry actions and tools.
  • Slide 11.3: Diagram — Draw request → review → approve, return or reject, then show agent access and tool access as separate gates.
  • Slide 11.4: Decision method — compare the correct control with the lesson distractors.
  • Slide 11.5: Demonstration — use LAB-11; show baseline, scope and result.
  • Slide 11.6: Failure investigation — Equating installed with approved for everyone, registry inventory with all possible agents, or view permission with management permission.
  • Slide 11.7: Retrieval check — Why is a registry owner not proof that the agent has safe tools? Answer: accountability and permitted capabilities are different properties.
  • Slide 11.8: Practice and transfer — submit Reconciled registry, publication decision and tool-control test. Pass requires matching containment to the actual capability.

Deck segment M12 — Agent security and compliance operations

  • Slide 12.1: Business problem — use the first lesson scenario; ask for an initial prediction.
  • Slide 12.2: Concepts — explain Agent activity monitoring, Agent data protection, Agent compliance gaps.
  • Slide 12.3: Diagram — Draw source → agent → tool → destination, with identity and data-control checkpoints; add an evidence-to-requirement compliance table.
  • Slide 12.4: Decision method — compare the correct control with the lesson distractors.
  • Slide 12.5: Demonstration — use LAB-12; show baseline, scope and result.
  • Slide 12.6: Failure investigation — Treating approval as permanent safety, prompt wording as enforcement and product availability as organizational compliance.
  • Slide 12.7: Retrieval check — Why is a tenfold activity spike not proof of compromise? Answer: legitimate demand, retries or a loop can also explain it.
  • Slide 12.8: Practice and transfer — submit Activity investigation, data-flow control map and compliance evidence matrix. Pass requires an evidence-backed reason for each status.

Deck segment M13 — AI economics, adoption and service health

  • Slide 13.1: Business problem — use the first lesson scenario; ask for an initial prediction.
  • Slide 13.2: Concepts — explain AI cost reconciliation, Adoption and AI service health.
  • Slide 13.3: Diagram — Draw three columns: entitlement, activity and accepted outcomes. Add a cost-reconciliation table with overlap removal.
  • Slide 13.4: Decision method — compare the correct control with the lesson distractors.
  • Slide 13.5: Demonstration — use LAB-13; show baseline, scope and result.
  • Slide 13.6: Failure investigation — Double-counting cost surfaces, comparing unequal report windows and equating prompts with productivity.
  • Slide 13.7: Retrieval check — What is adoption for 40 active users among 100 licensed users? Answer: 40 percent for that defined population and period; it is not a value claim.
  • Slide 13.8: Practice and transfer — submit Reconciled cost ledger and operational report. Pass requires the correct denominator, overlap treatment and a distinction between adoption and value.

Revision guide and exam priorities

Microsoft does not publish the frequency of individual live questions. The priorities below are inferred from domain weight and conceptual risk, not leaked or measured item frequency. Security/governance and AI deserve most study time. Do not omit the smaller tenant domain because its concepts underpin later scenarios.

PriorityWhy candidates struggleRevision action
Control boundariesAccess, discovery, licensing, billing and labels can look like interchangeable toggles.Explain each boundary with an allowed and denied example.
Policy interactionCandidates reason from one policy or one prompt rather than effective evaluation.Work policy truth tables and inspect event evidence.
Purview requirementsClassification, protection, DLP, retention and recovery answer different questions.Choose data shape, location, action and time before naming a feature.
Agent subject and ownershipBlueprints, child identities, user accounts and registry owners are distinct.Draw objects and token subjects before scoping policies.
New product behaviorCowork controls, connectors and agent capabilities can evolve quickly.Recheck current product pages and note the verified date.
Operational evidenceSaved configuration or a green dashboard is mistaken for an outcome.Collect positive, negative and post-change evidence.

Beginner challenges: vocabulary overload, understanding workload boundaries, interpreting role scope, reading logs and distinguishing authentication from authorization. Remediation: redo the foundation bridge and explain a single scenario without product jargon. Advanced concepts: multi-policy evaluation, identity-layer targeting, data-flow enforcement, propagation effects, mixed billing reconciliation and compliance evidence. Extension: analyze a failure where the first plausible explanation is wrong.

Exam cram — last review session

  • A license permits a product; a role permits administration; a billing arrangement funds supported consumption.
  • Verify direct group membership and effective user assignments; do not assume nested licensing.
  • TXT ownership verification is separate from MX cutover.
  • Mailbox read and send permissions are distinct; channel membership can differ from team membership.
  • Allowed transcription is not an existing transcript; verify the meeting state.
  • Missing discovery is not proof of denied access. Test a direct URL with the right identity.
  • Eligible PIM role is not an active permanent assignment.
  • An applicable block survives a successful MFA requirement elsewhere.
  • No fresh MFA prompt can still be consistent with valid policy enforcement.
  • Identify the threat-protection feature and applicable policy source before comparing priority.
  • Classification identifies content; labels configure classification/protection; DLP governs supported actions; retention preserves/disposes; backup recovers.
  • A blocked DLP attempt is not automatically a successful leak.
  • Correct source oversharing before expanding AI retrieval.
  • Agent blueprint, child identity and agent user account are different policy subjects.
  • Inventory, approval, access and tool invocation each need their own evidence.
  • Cowork access and visibility are separate; verify current spending-policy behavior.
  • No indexing does not mean no runtime transfer; test connector identities and permissions.
  • Count overlapping charges once and define adoption denominators.
  • Read the question for scope, required action, constraints and evidence; reject an option that solves a different problem.
  • Review actual appointment instructions. Do not infer exam timing, lab presence or allowed resources from this workbook.

30-day study plan — 60 hours

30Readiness reviewCram guide; timed unseen Learn exercises where available; booking-rule check

The first 55 scheduled hours cover foundation and modules, including embedded revision. The final five hours across days 28–30 are the formal case and readiness assessment; the remaining hour revisits weak module material. On each ordinary day use roughly 45 minutes of explanation, 45 of practical work and 30 of retrieval or error-log review; adjust to the task. Workshops are optional additional time. If a gate fails, extend the calendar rather than cram through it.

Readiness decision

Local mastery gates: at least 95% overall and at least 90% in each domain on both mixed checks, completed on separate days without notes or prior answer-key review; at least 16/20 on each integrated case; satisfactory lab evidence; and correct explanation of critical control distinctions. A 30-item check requires 29 correct answers to exceed 95%. Repeated or previously exposed questions are retrieval practice, not independent evidence. These gates are course design choices, not a validated exam prediction. Report live lab competence separately from fixture reasoning.

Flashcards — 60 prompts

FC01 — Branding

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Branding gives users recognizable organization and sign-in experiences; it does not establish trust in a message or authorize access. Evidence: Capture the organization experience and a fresh sign-in view using the pilot identity, checking the intended URLs and imagery.

FC02 — Domain verification

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Domain ownership verification and mail routing are separate decisions. Proving control of a namespace does not itself move email. Evidence: Check verified domain status and independently confirm the production MX destination still matches the approved pre-cutover configuration.

FC03 — Organization settings

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: A tenant contains organization-wide service choices and profile information; each setting needs a business owner and an understood scope. Evidence: Compare the saved values and effective experience against the approved requirements, including any propagation delay.

FC04 — Group licensing

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: A license is an entitlement assembled from service plans. Group assignment must be verified for the intended users rather than inferred from membership somewhere in a hierarchy. Evidence: Inspect direct membership, user-level entitlement sources, service-plan state and assignment errors for the ten affected accounts.

FC05 — AI entitlements and funding

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Product access, permission to create agents and the mechanism that pays for consumption answer different questions. Evidence: Produce a matrix of persona, product, role, billing source and available capacity, verified against current subscription details.

FC06 — Backup and recovery

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Backup prepares a recoverable copy or restore point for supported workloads; retention governs preservation and deletion obligations. Evidence: Compare restored test files with known expected contents, review job status and document missing or changed items.

FC07 — Network diagnosis

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Connectivity is a path from client through local network and security intermediaries to a cloud service. A healthy service can still be unreachable from one office. Evidence: Correlate timestamps, connectivity results and proxy observations, then retest the same pilot operation after the targeted fix.

FC08 — Service health

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Service health reports provider incidents and advisories; local symptom reports still need correlation before attribution. Evidence: Record matching or nonmatching workload, region, time window and incident details alongside pilot tests.

FC09 — Shared mailbox permissions

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Opening a mailbox and sending with its identity require different delegated permissions. Evidence: Send a synthetic message through the delegate and inspect the recipient-visible sender, while confirming existing read access.

FC10 — Teams membership

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Teams owners manage the workspace; channel membership and associated content boundaries depend on the channel type. Evidence: Test discussion and associated file access with an included and an excluded pilot identity.

FC11 — Meeting Copilot and transcripts

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Meeting policy, organizer options and the existence of a transcript jointly determine the experience. Allowing transcription is not evidence that it ran. Evidence: Check effective meeting settings and the actual saved transcript and post-meeting experience with an eligible licensed participant.

FC12 — SharePoint permissions

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Access is the result of effective permissions, group membership and sharing links, not the folder's reassuring name. Evidence: Use direct URLs with a finance identity and a nonfinance identity, recording an allow and a deny after the change.

FC13 — Discovery versus access

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Restricted discovery reduces broad surfacing; access restrictions decide who can open content. These controls need different tests. Evidence: Record direct access and broad discovery separately; account for indexing delay and documented discovery exceptions.

FC14 — User lifecycle

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: A user identity needs controlled creation, change and retirement. Departure handling must consider sessions, data and business ownership. Evidence: Verify access revocation and successor access separately, with a record of preserved data and remaining dependencies.

FC15 — Guests and external collaboration

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: A guest can participate through a governed external identity; external chat capability is not equivalent to membership or file access. Evidence: Test the intended resource and a prohibited resource with the guest, and record the review date.

FC16 — Group purpose

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Security groups and Microsoft 365 groups support different management and collaboration needs. Membership changes can affect multiple connected resources. Evidence: Inspect the group type, owners, membership and the expected connected resources.

FC17 — Privileged roles and PIM

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Least privilege limits capability; time-bound activation reduces how long privileged capability remains available. Evidence: Inspect activation duration, approval or justification evidence and the role state after expiry.

FC18 — Administrative units

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: An administrative unit can scope supported delegated directory administration; it is not a separate tenant or a universal data boundary. Evidence: Test a permitted operation on an in-scope test user and its rejection on an out-of-scope test user.

FC19 — Contacts

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: A mail contact represents an external recipient in the directory; it is not an interactive employee account. Evidence: Verify address-book resolution and the external delivery address without creating sign-in access.

FC20 — Graph PowerShell bulk administration

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Graph PowerShell exposes directory operations as commands. Safe bulk work uses explicit scope, validated input and per-record results. Evidence: Reconcile input targets with successful, failed and skipped records, and sample the resulting directory state.

FC21 — Authentication methods

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Authentication establishes identity. Method availability, registration and actual use must be distinguished. Evidence: Inspect the effective method policy, registration status and authentication details for the pilot sign-in.

FC22 — Password Protection

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Password Protection reduces weak password choices using prohibited-password evaluation; it does not replace multifactor authentication. Evidence: Record that prohibited test choices are rejected and acceptable synthetic choices work, without logging passwords.

FC23 — Self-service password reset

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: SSPR requires an eligible, in-scope user with sufficient usable registered methods. Configuration and readiness are separate. Evidence: Complete a controlled reset for the test identity and inspect the reset or audit evidence.

FC24 — Sign-in investigation

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: A missing MFA prompt does not establish a bypass; an existing authentication claim can satisfy the requirement. Evidence: Capture the relevant sign-in ID and explain each applicable policy's result and how authentication was satisfied.

FC25 — Conditional Access interaction

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Applicable Conditional Access requirements are cumulative; an applicable block is not canceled by another policy's grant. Evidence: Inspect the final blocked result and the contribution of each applicable policy for the same token request.

FC26 — Risk-based access

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Sign-in risk concerns a particular authentication event; user risk concerns potential compromise of the identity. Remediation must match the signal and supported controls. Evidence: Record the risk type, matched policy, intended action and observed test outcome without assuming every risk means confirmed compromise.

FC27 — Alert triage

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: An alert is a signal requiring assessment; its status is not the same as proof that the underlying threat was removed. Evidence: Link the alert to message or incident evidence and the recorded remediation result.

FC28 — Threat policy scope

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Threat-protection settings must match the feature and recipients. A policy's name does not prove effective coverage. Evidence: Confirm the applicable protection feature, protected identities or domains where relevant and effective recipients.

FC29 — Threat policy precedence

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Custom policy priority orders comparable custom policies; applicable preset protection can take precedence for a feature. Evidence: Identify the actual policy source for the feature and explain why it wins for that recipient.

FC30 — Threat investigation and response

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Investigation connects evidence to affected assets; response must record what action was taken and whether it worked. Evidence: Correlate message identifiers, recipient evidence, click or sign-in signals where available and the result of each remediation action.

FC31 — Attack simulation training

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: A simulation is a controlled learning activity with approved scope and follow-up; it is not permission to send uncontrolled phishing. Evidence: Compare reporting and repeat-behavior measures across the planned exercise and follow-up, noting cohort differences.

FC32 — Classification techniques

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Pattern detection, matching known records and recognizing document meaning address different classification problems. Evidence: Test known records, lookalike nonrecords and realistic benign content, recording false positives and false negatives.

FC33 — Sensitivity labels and publishing

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: A sensitivity label describes and can protect content; a publishing policy makes labels available to the intended users. Encryption depends on configuration. Evidence: Use an in-scope account to label test content and an excluded account to verify the intended publishing boundary.

FC34 — DLP requirements

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: A DLP requirement connects a location, detection condition and supported action. One workload's action should not be assumed for all others. Evidence: Test matching external mail, permitted internal mail and benign external mail; inspect policy events and user experience.

FC35 — Copilot DLP boundaries

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Copilot DLP must use supported scenario-specific conditions and actions; it does not automatically govern every agent interaction. Evidence: Record the relevant policy scope, labelled test item and processing behavior; distinguish a citation from use of item content.

FC36 — Retention design

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Retention rules govern preservation and disposal over time. Labels can express item-specific needs within a broader location policy. Evidence: Document the record's applicable retain actions, start event and expected earliest ordinary disposal time.

FC37 — DLP alert response

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: A DLP alert needs context about policy, item, actor and action before a responder can judge exposure and tune controls. Evidence: Record attempted versus completed activity, blocked versus allowed outcome and any justified follow-up.

FC38 — DSPM for AI

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Data security posture management identifies and prioritizes data risks; findings need owners, remediation and later verification. Evidence: Compare targeted before-and-after access findings and direct tests, noting assessment refresh timing.

FC39 — Copilot readiness

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Readiness combines identity, entitlement, supported clients, service configuration and operational support. A purchased seat is only one component. Evidence: Maintain a pass, fail or untested checklist for prerequisites and complete a representative pilot task.

FC40 — Data readiness

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: AI can make already-accessible information easier to find; existing oversharing therefore matters before rollout. Evidence: Prove the affected user's direct access changes as intended and then retest representative retrieval after propagation.

FC41 — User experiences

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Adoption support should target actual job tasks, supported experiences and user feedback while preserving controls. Evidence: Review workload-level activity together with task completion and user-reported quality, not merely seat counts.

FC42 — Phased rollout decisions

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: A pilot is an experiment with acceptance criteria, not a ceremony before a predetermined launch. Evidence: Re-run the failed access and escalation scenarios and record approval against the same launch criteria.

FC43 — Copilot web search

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Web grounding and work-data grounding have different information boundaries. Policy scope must be checked in the effective experience. Evidence: Inspect the effective policy assignment and test relevant modes after policy retrieval, recording uncertainty where runtime output is inconclusive.

FC44 — Copilot Search

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Search configuration influences finding permitted information; permission and source quality still govern useful results. Evidence: Compare a known permitted item and a known denied item, documenting source access and discovery independently.

FC45 — Tenant Copilot settings

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: AI tenant settings need distinct decisions for purchasing, admin experiences, releases, disclaimers and generation features. Evidence: Capture the effective settings and test the intended user experiences; note any setting that lacks the desired granularity.

FC46 — Cowork administration

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Cowork access, discovery, model availability and web-search behavior are separate controls. Current access administration must be checked against current documentation. Evidence: Review all applicable spending policies plus product prerequisites, and distinguish effective access from discoverability.

FC47 — Providers and connectors

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: External AI and source integrations need explicit data-flow and identity analysis; connector models differ in indexing and query-time retrieval. Evidence: Trace a synthetic record through the documented flow and test both authorized retrieval and the source or indexed access boundary.

FC48 — Agent identity lifecycle

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: An agent identity needs accountable ownership from creation through retirement. Its sponsor's departure is an operational event. Evidence: Inspect the resulting sponsor or retirement state and verify required access changes and audit evidence.

FC49 — Agent access governance

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: An agent's access should match its task and resource boundaries; supported access packages and policy controls need deliberate targeting. Evidence: Validate a required operation and a prohibited operation under the actual agent identity, then confirm expiry or removal behavior.

FC50 — Agent ownership layers

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Business sponsorship, technical identity ownership and registry ownership need not be the same person or object. Evidence: Produce an ownership matrix tied to the relevant objects and verify that each accountable person accepts the responsibility.

FC51 — Agent Conditional Access

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Agent-token policy evaluation depends on the subject, resource and supported controls; agents cannot satisfy a human authentication prompt. Evidence: Use fresh token-request evidence for included and excluded identities after propagation, and assess the separate user-account policy.

FC52 — Agent settings

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Tenant agent settings shape permitted creation, sharing, templates and user availability; these are governance choices with different scopes. Evidence: Record effective settings and both permitted and prohibited sharing or access attempts.

FC53 — Agent inventory

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: A registry enables discovery and curation, but inventory visibility depends on integration and discovery coverage. Evidence: Maintain a reconciled inventory noting source platform, registry state, owner and uncovered agents.

FC54 — Agent publication review

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Publication is a governance decision that should evaluate purpose, ownership, permissions, data flows and evidence. Evidence: Keep the review decision, required changes and verification evidence attached to the specific version or request.

FC55 — Registry actions and tools

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Agent availability and the tools it can invoke are related but distinct control surfaces. Evidence: Observe the blocked invocation and continued permitted behavior, with corresponding governance records.

FC56 — Agent activity monitoring

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Monitoring links an agent's identity and actions to expected behavior, owners and anomalies. Counts alone do not establish safety. Evidence: Document the activity pattern and root-cause evidence, plus the effect of any containment or correction.

FC57 — Agent data protection

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Agent data protection must match the actual workload and action path; a broad AI label is not a universal enforcement guarantee. Evidence: Test a synthetic sensitive outbound operation and a benign permitted operation under the actual agent execution path.

FC58 — Agent compliance gaps

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: A compliance finding is a gap between required controls and observed evidence, not merely an unfavorable dashboard color. Evidence: Link each requirement to a configured control, tested outcome, owner and unresolved limitation.

FC59 — AI cost reconciliation

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Cost reporting needs consistent periods, billing boundaries and units; overlapping views must not be added as separate charges. Evidence: Produce a reconciled ledger with unique charges, overlaps, excluded taxes or credits and the reporting period.

FC60 — Adoption and AI service health

Front: What distinction matters most, and how would you prove the result?

Back: Usage, useful outcomes and service reliability are different measures. Healthy adoption reporting defines the population and time window. Evidence: Reconcile the report population and period, compare workload-level use and inspect matching service-health evidence.

Glossary — 60 working terms

AI cost reconciliation — Cost reporting needs consistent periods, billing boundaries and units; overlapping views must not be added as separate charges.

AI entitlements and funding — Product access, permission to create agents and the mechanism that pays for consumption answer different questions.

Administrative units — An administrative unit can scope supported delegated directory administration; it is not a separate tenant or a universal data boundary.

Adoption and AI service health — Usage, useful outcomes and service reliability are different measures. Healthy adoption reporting defines the population and time window.

Agent Conditional Access — Agent-token policy evaluation depends on the subject, resource and supported controls; agents cannot satisfy a human authentication prompt.

Agent access governance — An agent's access should match its task and resource boundaries; supported access packages and policy controls need deliberate targeting.

Agent activity monitoring — Monitoring links an agent's identity and actions to expected behavior, owners and anomalies. Counts alone do not establish safety.

Agent compliance gaps — A compliance finding is a gap between required controls and observed evidence, not merely an unfavorable dashboard color.

Agent data protection — Agent data protection must match the actual workload and action path; a broad AI label is not a universal enforcement guarantee.

Agent identity lifecycle — An agent identity needs accountable ownership from creation through retirement. Its sponsor's departure is an operational event.

Agent inventory — A registry enables discovery and curation, but inventory visibility depends on integration and discovery coverage.

Agent ownership layers — Business sponsorship, technical identity ownership and registry ownership need not be the same person or object.

Agent publication review — Publication is a governance decision that should evaluate purpose, ownership, permissions, data flows and evidence.

Agent settings — Tenant agent settings shape permitted creation, sharing, templates and user availability; these are governance choices with different scopes.

Alert triage — An alert is a signal requiring assessment; its status is not the same as proof that the underlying threat was removed.

Attack simulation training — A simulation is a controlled learning activity with approved scope and follow-up; it is not permission to send uncontrolled phishing.

Authentication methods — Authentication establishes identity. Method availability, registration and actual use must be distinguished.

Backup and recovery — Backup prepares a recoverable copy or restore point for supported workloads; retention governs preservation and deletion obligations.

Branding — Branding gives users recognizable organization and sign-in experiences; it does not establish trust in a message or authorize access.

Classification techniques — Pattern detection, matching known records and recognizing document meaning address different classification problems.

Conditional Access interaction — Applicable Conditional Access requirements are cumulative; an applicable block is not canceled by another policy's grant.

Contacts — A mail contact represents an external recipient in the directory; it is not an interactive employee account.

Copilot DLP boundaries — Copilot DLP must use supported scenario-specific conditions and actions; it does not automatically govern every agent interaction.

Copilot Search — Search configuration influences finding permitted information; permission and source quality still govern useful results.

Copilot readiness — Readiness combines identity, entitlement, supported clients, service configuration and operational support. A purchased seat is only one component.

Copilot web search — Web grounding and work-data grounding have different information boundaries. Policy scope must be checked in the effective experience.

Cowork administration — Cowork access, discovery, model availability and web-search behavior are separate controls. Current access administration must be checked against current documentation.

DLP alert response — A DLP alert needs context about policy, item, actor and action before a responder can judge exposure and tune controls.

DLP requirements — A DLP requirement connects a location, detection condition and supported action. One workload's action should not be assumed for all others.

DSPM for AI — Data security posture management identifies and prioritizes data risks; findings need owners, remediation and later verification.

Data readiness — AI can make already-accessible information easier to find; existing oversharing therefore matters before rollout.

Discovery versus access — Restricted discovery reduces broad surfacing; access restrictions decide who can open content. These controls need different tests.

Domain verification — Domain ownership verification and mail routing are separate decisions. Proving control of a namespace does not itself move email.

Graph PowerShell bulk administration — Graph PowerShell exposes directory operations as commands. Safe bulk work uses explicit scope, validated input and per-record results.

Group licensing — A license is an entitlement assembled from service plans. Group assignment must be verified for the intended users rather than inferred from membership somewhere in a hierarchy.

Group purpose — Security groups and Microsoft 365 groups support different management and collaboration needs. Membership changes can affect multiple connected resources.

Guests and external collaboration — A guest can participate through a governed external identity; external chat capability is not equivalent to membership or file access.

Meeting Copilot and transcripts — Meeting policy, organizer options and the existence of a transcript jointly determine the experience. Allowing transcription is not evidence that it ran.

Network diagnosis — Connectivity is a path from client through local network and security intermediaries to a cloud service. A healthy service can still be unreachable from one office.

Organization settings — A tenant contains organization-wide service choices and profile information; each setting needs a business owner and an understood scope.

Password Protection — Password Protection reduces weak password choices using prohibited-password evaluation; it does not replace multifactor authentication.

Phased rollout decisions — A pilot is an experiment with acceptance criteria, not a ceremony before a predetermined launch.

Privileged roles and PIM — Least privilege limits capability; time-bound activation reduces how long privileged capability remains available.

Providers and connectors — External AI and source integrations need explicit data-flow and identity analysis; connector models differ in indexing and query-time retrieval.

Registry actions and tools — Agent availability and the tools it can invoke are related but distinct control surfaces.

Retention design — Retention rules govern preservation and disposal over time. Labels can express item-specific needs within a broader location policy.

Risk-based access — Sign-in risk concerns a particular authentication event; user risk concerns potential compromise of the identity. Remediation must match the signal and supported controls.

Self-service password reset — SSPR requires an eligible, in-scope user with sufficient usable registered methods. Configuration and readiness are separate.

Sensitivity labels and publishing — A sensitivity label describes and can protect content; a publishing policy makes labels available to the intended users. Encryption depends on configuration.

Service health — Service health reports provider incidents and advisories; local symptom reports still need correlation before attribution.

SharePoint permissions — Access is the result of effective permissions, group membership and sharing links, not the folder's reassuring name.

Shared mailbox permissions — Opening a mailbox and sending with its identity require different delegated permissions.

Sign-in investigation — A missing MFA prompt does not establish a bypass; an existing authentication claim can satisfy the requirement.

Teams membership — Teams owners manage the workspace; channel membership and associated content boundaries depend on the channel type.

Tenant Copilot settings — AI tenant settings need distinct decisions for purchasing, admin experiences, releases, disclaimers and generation features.

Threat investigation and response — Investigation connects evidence to affected assets; response must record what action was taken and whether it worked.

Threat policy precedence — Custom policy priority orders comparable custom policies; applicable preset protection can take precedence for a feature.

Threat policy scope — Threat-protection settings must match the feature and recipients. A policy's name does not prove effective coverage.

User experiences — Adoption support should target actual job tasks, supported experiences and user feedback while preserving controls.

User lifecycle — A user identity needs controlled creation, change and retirement. Departure handling must consider sessions, data and business ownership.

300 reinforcement activities — reference and answers

Open the original 300 activities and answer explanations

Attempt first, then open the explanation. Related activities reinforce the same objectives; this is not a calibrated exam simulator.

Q001 — Scenario single choice

L01 | D1.T1.1

A newly acquired division needs its logo, company URL, theme and sign-in background reviewed before launch. Which action best meets the requirement?

  1. Change only the email signature
  2. Review Microsoft 365 organization branding and Entra sign-in branding separately, then test both experiences.
  3. Assign a security group to the theme
  4. Use the logo as evidence that a sign-in is genuine
Answer and explanation

B

A: Incorrect. Signatures do not configure either tenant branding experience. B: Correct. Branding gives users recognizable organization and sign-in experiences; it does not establish trust in a message or authorize access. C: Incorrect. Group membership is not the branding configuration. D: Incorrect. Attackers can reproduce visual branding.

Q002 — Evidence single choice

L01 | D1.T1.1

After addressing this case: A newly acquired division needs its logo, company URL, theme and sign-in background reviewed before launch. Which evidence is strongest for closing the change?

  1. Capture the organization experience and a fresh sign-in view using the pilot identity, checking the intended URLs and imagery.
  2. The administrator can complete a similar task with an elevated account.
  3. The change ticket is approved and the configuration page shows Saved.
  4. One pilot user reports that the general experience looks normal.
Answer and explanation

A

A: Correct. This tests the stated control at the relevant scope and outcome. B: Incorrect. An administrator may have different access and policy scope from the actual pilot identity; this is not a representative outcome test. C: Incorrect. Authorization and saved configuration do not prove that the intended operation reached the right objects or was enforced. D: Incorrect. A general report lacks the specific resource, identity, action and expected negative test needed to validate this requirement.

Q003 — Multiple response — choose two

L01 | D1.T1.1

  1. Change only the email signature
  2. Assign a security group to the theme
  3. Review Microsoft 365 organization branding and Entra sign-in branding separately, then test both experiences.
  4. Capture the organization experience and a fresh sign-in view using the pilot identity, checking the intended URLs and imagery.
  5. Use the logo as evidence that a sign-in is genuine
Answer and explanation

C, D

A: Incorrect. Signatures do not configure either tenant branding experience. B: Incorrect. Group membership is not the branding configuration. C: Correct. Branding gives users recognizable organization and sign-in experiences; it does not establish trust in a message or authorize access. D: Correct. This supplies the control-specific verification required before claiming success. E: Incorrect. Attackers can reproduce visual branding.

Q004 — Matching / drag-and-drop equivalent

L01 | D1.T1.1

Match each concept to its description. Use every answer once. Concepts: 1. Branding; 2. Administrative units; 3. DSPM for AI

  1. An administrative unit can scope supported delegated directory administration; it is not a separate tenant or a universal data boundary.
  2. Branding gives users recognizable organization and sign-in experiences; it does not establish trust in a message or authorize access.
  3. Data security posture management identifies and prioritizes data risks; findings need owners, remediation and later verification.
Answer and explanation

1 → B; 2 → A; 3 → C

Branding: Branding gives users recognizable organization and sign-in experiences; it does not establish trust in a message or authorize access. Administrative units: An administrative unit can scope supported delegated directory administration; it is not a separate tenant or a universal data boundary. DSPM for AI: Data security posture management identifies and prioritizes data risks; findings need owners, remediation and later verification. The distinction is the purpose and scope of each control, not a shared product label.

Q005 — Case review — constructed response

L01 | D1.T1.1

Northstar change review: A newly acquired division needs its logo, company URL, theme and sign-in background reviewed before launch. A colleague proposes: Change only the email signature. In 100–150 words, explain why this does or does not meet the requirement; recommend a scoped correction, describe outcome evidence, and identify a rollback or limitation. Do not invent an observed successful test.

    Answer and explanation

    Rubric: requirement 1; correction 1; rejection rationale 1; verification 1; rollback/limitation 1.

    Model response: Signatures do not configure either tenant branding experience. Review Microsoft 365 organization branding and Entra sign-in branding separately, then test both experiences. Branding gives users recognizable organization and sign-in experiences; it does not establish trust in a message or authorize access. Verify as follows: Capture the organization experience and a fresh sign-in view using the pilot identity, checking the intended URLs and imagery. Record the baseline and reverse only the disposable scoped change if acceptance fails; for irreversible, retention-protected or chargeable operations, state the limitation and obtain the normal owner decision before action. Award the final point only for a limitation or rollback relevant to this case, not a generic claim that everything can be undone.

    Q006 — Scenario single choice

    L02 | D1.T1.2

    A company owns a new domain but must keep its existing mail provider until a scheduled migration. Which action best meets the requirement?

    1. Rename every account first
    2. Add the domain and publish the supplied verification record; defer mail-routing changes until the agreed cutover.
    3. Change MX records immediately
    4. Create a SharePoint site with the domain name
    Answer and explanation

    B

    A: Incorrect. Verification should precede use of an unverified namespace. B: Correct. Domain ownership verification and mail routing are separate decisions. Proving control of a namespace does not itself move email. C: Incorrect. This can move mail before the service is ready. D: Incorrect. A site name does not prove DNS ownership.

        Q016 — Scenario single choice

        L04 | D1.T1.4

        A licensed parent group contains 20 direct users and a nested group of 10 users. Only the direct users receive the product. Which action best meets the requirement?

        1. Assign the entitlement to an appropriate group containing the intended direct user members and inspect assignment results.
        2. Enable an unrelated service plan
        3. Wait for nested membership to inherit the license
        4. Treat group success as proof all 30 users are licensed
        Answer and explanation

        A

        A: Correct. A license is an entitlement assembled from service plans. Group assignment must be verified for the intended users rather than inferred from membership somewhere in a hierarchy. B: Incorrect. The missing assignment is a membership-scope issue. C: Incorrect. Nested groups are not processed by group-based licensing. D: Incorrect. Success covers processed users, not nested users.

        Q017 — Evidence single choice

        L04 | D1.T1.4

          Q021 — Scenario single choice

          L05 | D1.T1.5

          Eight makers build agents for 80 occasional users. Procurement asks whether maker access alone funds all user consumption. Which action best meets the requirement?

          1. Separate the maker roles, user product entitlements and supported consumption funding, then validate the required licensing combination.
          2. Use an agent owner field as billing authorization
          3. Assign every user an administrator role
          4. Assume a maker role pays for all usage
          Answer and explanation

          A

          A: Correct. Product access, permission to create agents and the mechanism that pays for consumption answer different questions. B: Incorrect. Ownership and funding are separate controls. C: Incorrect. Administrative permission neither replaces licensing nor justifies broad access. D: Incorrect. An administrative or maker permission is not a billing arrangement.

          Q022 — Evidence single choice

          L05 | D1.T1.5

An administrator may have different access and policy scope from the actual pilot identity; this is not a representative outcome test.

          L05 | D1.T1.5

          Match each concept to its description. Use every answer once. Concepts: 1. AI entitlements and funding; 2. Password Protection; 3. Phased rollout decisions

          1. Product access, permission to create agents and the mechanism that pays for consumption answer different questions.

          Answer and explanation

          1 → A; 2 → B; 3 → C

          AI entitlements and funding: Product access, permission to create agents and the mechanism that pays for consumption answer different questions. Password Protection: Password Protection reduces weak password choices using prohibited-password evaluation; it does not replace multifactor authentication. Phased rollout decisions: A pilot is an experiment with acceptance criteria, not a ceremony before a predetermined launch. The distinction is the purpose and scope of each control, not a shared product label.

          Q025 — Case review — constructed response

          L05 | D1.T1.5

          Northstar change review: Eight makers build agents for 80 occasional users. Procurement asks whether maker access alone funds all user consumption. A colleague proposes: Assume a maker role pays for all usage. In 100–150 words, explain why this does or does not meet the requirement; recommend a scoped correction, describe outcome evidence, and identify a rollback or limitation. Do not invent an observed successful test.

            Answer and explanation

            Rubric: requirement 1; correction 1; rejection rationale 1; verification 1; rollback/limitation 1.

            Model response: An administrative or maker permission is not a billing arrangement. Separate the maker roles, user product entitlements and supported consumption funding, then validate the required licensing combination. Product access, permission to create agents and the mechanism that pays for consumption answer different questions. Verify as follows: Produce a matrix of persona, product, role, billing source and available capacity, verified against current subscription details. Record the baseline and reverse only the disposable scoped change if acceptance fails; for irreversible, retention-protected or chargeable operations, state the limitation and obtain the normal owner decision before action. Award the final point only for a limitation or rollback relevant to this case, not a generic claim that everything can be undone.

            Q026 — Scenario single choice

            L06 | D1.T1.6

            A test library was damaged yesterday. The recovery owner needs a defensible restoration rather than simply a retention setting. Which action best meets the requirement?

            1. Close the incident when the restore is submitted
            2. Restore everything without identifying the target time
            3. Confirm protected scope and a suitable restore point in Microsoft 365 Backup, choose the recovery option and validate recovered content.
            4. Apply a new retention label and assume yesterday is restored
            Answer and explanation

            C

            A: Incorrect. Submission does not establish recovery success. B: Incorrect. An unnecessary broad restore can replace valid recent work. C: Correct. Backup prepares a recoverable copy or restore point for supported workloads; retention governs preservation and deletion obligations. D: Incorrect. Retention configuration does not perform point-in-time recovery.

            Q027 — Evidence single choice

            L06 | D1.T1.6

            After addressing this case: A test library was damaged yesterday. The recovery owner needs a defensible restoration rather than simply a retention setting. Which evidence is strongest for closing the change?

            3. Compare restored test files with known expected contents, review job status and document missing or changed items.
            4. The administrator can complete a similar task with an elevated account.
            Answer and explanation

            C

            A: Incorrect. Authorization and saved configuration do not prove that the intended operation reached the right objects or was enforced. B: Incorrect. A general report lacks the specific resource, identity, action and expected negative test needed to validate this requirement. C: Correct. This tests the stated control at the relevant scope and outcome. D: Incorrect. An administrator may have different access and policy scope from the actual pilot identity; this is not a representative outcome test.

              Q031 — Scenario single choice

Record the baseline and reverse only the disposable scoped change if acceptance fails; for irreversible, retention-protected or chargeable operations, state the limitation and obtain the normal owner decision before action. Award the final point only for a limitation or rollback relevant to this case, not a generic claim that everything can be undone.

                        Q056 — Scenario single choice

                        L12 | D1.W1.4

                        A finance site is called Private Finance but an all-staff group still has read access. Which action best meets the requirement?

                        1. Change the title to Highly Confidential
                        2. Exclude the site from search and declare the access fixed
                        3. Trace effective access and remove the unintended grant or link while preserving the finance team's required access.
                        4. Remove only a navigation link
                        Answer and explanation

                        C

                        A: Incorrect. A name does not alter authorization. B: Incorrect. Discovery suppression does not remove permissions. C: Correct. Access is the result of effective permissions, group membership and sharing links, not the folder's reassuring name. D: Incorrect. Users may still open saved URLs.

                          Q061 — Scenario single choice

                          L13 | D1.W1.5

                          A site should stay accessible to its permitted users by direct link but should not be broadly surfaced through organization-wide Copilot discovery. Which action best meets the requirement?

                          1. Remove every authorized user's permissions
                          2. Assume a missing search result proves access is denied
                          3. Use a site restriction as proof OneDrive is identically configured
                          4. Evaluate Restricted Content Discovery for the site and separately keep reviewing its underlying permissions.
                          Answer and explanation

                          D

                          A: Incorrect. That violates the requirement to preserve direct access. B: Incorrect. Discovery and authorization are separate observations. C: Incorrect. Control support and scope differ by workload. D: Correct. Restricted discovery reduces broad surfacing; access restrictions decide who can open content. These controls need different tests.

                                    Q086 — Scenario single choice

                                    L18 | D2.I1.5

                                    A regional helpdesk should administer supported user tasks for its region without gaining the same scope everywhere. Which action best meets the requirement?

                                    1. Use a tenant-wide role and ask staff to avoid other users
                                    2. Use an administrative unit and a supported role assignment scoped to that unit.
                                    3. Assume the unit isolates all SharePoint data
                                    4. Rename the region's users
                                    Answer and explanation

                                    B

                                    A: Incorrect. A written request is not enforced scoping. B: Correct.                                                   Q121 — Scenario single choice

                                                  L25 | D2.A1.5

                                                  One applicable policy requires MFA; a second applicable policy blocks the user's access to the same resource. Which action best meets the requirement?

                                                  1. Ignore resource targeting
                                                  2. Assume passing MFA overrides the block
                                                  3. Apply only the most recently edited policy
                                                  4. Predict blocked access and use the sign-in evidence to verify both policy evaluations.
                                                  Answer and explanation

                                                  D

                                                  A: Incorrect. Applicability depends on assignments and conditions. B: Incorrect. A grant does not negate an applicable block. C: Incorrect. Policies are not evaluated as a last-edit-wins list. D: Correct. Applicable Conditional Access requirements are cumulative; an applicable block is not canceled by another policy's grant.

                                                              Q151 — Scenario single choice

                                                              2. Send an unapproved simulation to all customers
                                                              3. Use real stolen credentials in the exercise
                                                              4. Plan an approved simulation for a bounded population with appropriate training and measurable follow-up.

A simulation is a controlled learning activity with approved scope and follow-up; it is not permission to send uncontrolled phishing.

                                                                        Answer and explanation

                                                                        C

                                                                        A: Incorrect. Sensitivity and retention labels serve different functions. B: Incorrect. An active retention requirement prevents ordinary permanent deletion. C: Correct. Retention rules govern preservation and disposal over time. Labels can express item-specific needs within a broader location policy. D: Incorrect. Recovery and lifecycle requirements are separate.
